CLT for β \beta -ensembles with Freud weights, application to the KLS conjecture in Schatten balls
Charlie Dworaczek Guera, Ronan Memin, Michel Pain

Abstract
In this paper, we are interested in the β \beta -ensembles (or 1D log-gas) with Freud weights, namely with a potential of the form | x | p \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p} with p ≥ 2 p\geq 2 . Since this potential is not of class 𝒞 3 \mathcal{C}^{3} when p ∈ ( 2 , 3 ] p\in(2,3] , most of the literature does not apply. In this singular setting, we prove the central limit theorem for linear statistics with general test-functions and compute the subleading correction to the free energy. Our strategy relies on establishing an optimal local law in the spirit of [ 19 ] . Our results allow us to give a large N N expansion up to o ⁡ ( N ) o(N) of the log-volume of the unit balls of N × N N\times N self-adjoint matrices for the p p -Schatten norms and to give a consistency check of the KLS conjecture. For the latter, we consider the functions OPEN f ⁡ ( X ) = Tr ⁡ ( X r CLOSE ) q f(X)=\mathrm{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q} and the uniform distributions on these same Schatten balls for N N large enough. While the case p > 3 p>3 , q = 1 , r = 2 q=1,r=2 , was proven in [ 24 ] , we address in the present paper the case p ≥ 2 p\geq 2 , q ≥ 1 q\geq 1 and r ≥ 2 r\geq 2 an even integer. The proofs are based on a link between the moments of norms of uniform laws on p p -Schatten balls and the β \beta -ensembles with Freud weights.



1
Introduction

1.1
Setting of the problem

Let
\beta>0
,
p>0
and
V(x)\coloneqq c_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p}
for a constant
c_{p}>0
to be fixed later. For any
N\geq 1
, the
\beta
-ensembles with Freud weights
is the probability distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
given by:

\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})\coloneqq\frac{1}{Z_{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}}\right\rvert^{\beta}\cdot e^{-\frac{\beta N}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}V(\lambda_{i})}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{N},

(1.1)

where the
partition function

Z_{N}
is defined by

Z_{N}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}}\right\rvert^{\beta}\cdot e^{-\frac{\beta N}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}V(\lambda_{i})}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{N}.

(1.2)

Note that we omit the dependence on
N
in the notation
\mathbb{P}
.
Results in this paper cover only the case
p\geq 2
.
For
\beta=1,2,4
and quadratic
V
, this law is realized by the (renormalized by
\sqrt{N}
) spectrum of the classical random matrix ensembles GOE, GUE and GSE. For general
\beta>0
, it is realized as the joint law of the eigenvalues of certain tridiagonal random matrices
[
26
,
42
]
. This model, for general
V
and
\beta
, has been extensively studied for decades. A central object in the study of the Gibbs measure
\mathbb{P}_{N}
is the empirical measure
\mu_{N}
defined by

\mu_{N}\coloneqq\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\lambda_{i}}.

(1.3)

For any continuous potential
V
such that
V(x)\geq(1+\varepsilon)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert
for
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert
large enough, the empirical measure converges weakly a.s. towards a deterministic limit
\mu_{V}
, called the
equilibrium measure
which is the unique minimizer among all probability measures
\mu
of the energy functional:

\mathcal{E}(\mu)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}V(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x-y}}\right\rvert\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(y),

(1.4)

see
[
20
,
34
]
, as well as
[
11
,
3
]
for a large deviation approach.
Under the Freud potential
V(x)=c_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p}
with
p>0
, the measure
\mu_{V}
is known, see
[
24
, Lemma 4.1]
which is slightly rewritten from
[
53
, Theorem IV.6.1]
: it is supported on
[-1,1]
, provided we choose

c_{p}\coloneqq\dfrac{\Gamma(\frac{p}{2})\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{p+1}{2})},

(1.5)

and it is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, with density given by:

\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}}{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}(x)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert\leq 1}\dfrac{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p-1}}{\pi}\int_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert}^{1}\dfrac{y^{-p}}{\sqrt{1-y^{2}}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y.

(1.6)

This measure is known as the
Ullman
distribution, see
[
53
, IV.5.1]
or
[
36
]
.

A natural question, following the convergence of
\mu_{N}
, is to investigate the nature of the fluctuations of the empirical measure around its limit
\mu_{V}
, specifically by establishing a
central limit theorem
(CLT). The relevant object for this purpose is:

L_{N}\coloneqq N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{N}-\mu_{V}}}\right).

(1.7)

It can be shown that this object is asymptotically Gaussian in the following sense: for any smooth bounded function
f
:

L_{N}(f)\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{{\mathrm{law}}}{\mathrm{Gaussian}}.

(1.8)

Such a CLT has been first established in
[
34
]
and has been then extended to various settings (with different assumptions on
V
and
f
, or
f,\beta
dependent on
N
), see Subsection
1.3
.
However, all known results in the literature rely on regularity assumptions on
V
. For instance, the most refined result
[
9
]
requires
V\in\mathcal{C}^{6}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{4}
.

In our setting,
i.e.
the Freud potential with
p\geq 2
,
V
is in general only
\mathcal{C}^{2}
and not
\mathcal{C}^{3}
at 0.
The main goal of this article is to establish such a CLT for this model, for a general test-function
f
and for this potential that is smooth everywhere except at one point, where it presents a singularity (whose third derivative is locally integrable). As a corollary, we will also compute the subleading-order term of the
free energy

\log Z_{N}
. One of the main ingredients of the proof is to establish an optimal
local law
.
Our motivation is twofold. Firstly, we aim at developing a new approach to prove CLT for more singular potentials.
Secondly, the particular choice of Freud weights has applications in asymptotic convex geometry: indeed, proving a central limit theorem for
f(x)=x^{r}
suffices to give a check on the so-called
Kannan–Lovász–Simonovits conjecture
for
p
-Schatten
unit balls with
p\geq 2
, as detailed below in Subsection
1.2.2
.

This CLT has already been obtained when
p>3
and with
f(x)=x^{2}
in
[
24
]
, motivated by applications in convex geometry.
Their proof follows closely
[
9
]
with some local improvements required because
V
is only
\mathcal{C}^{3}
in their case: the fact that the equilibrium measure is explicit shows that it has two additional levels of regularity compared to what is proved for general
\mathcal{C}^{3}
potential in
[
9
, Lemma 3.1]
, and the fact that the test function
f
considered is even results in gaining one level of regularity for the inverse of
f
via the master operator.
This strategy would not work for the case
p\in(2,3]
considered here, or even for
p\in(3,4]
but
f
not even.
Our approach is more self-contained, importing mainly the
a priori
bounds and controls of outliers stated in Section
5.1
.
Moreover, the fact of working with the specific test function
f(x)=x^{2}
in
[
24
]
is not enough to deduce the subleading term of the free energy that we obtain here.

1.2
Main results

1.2.1
About
\beta
-ensembles

Our main result is a central limit theorem, in the sense of the convergence of moments, for the linear statistics of
\mathcal{C}^{3}
functions having at most exponential growth.

Theorem 1.1

(CLT)
.

Let
p\geq 2
, and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
with at most exponential growth,
i.e.
there exists
C>0
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert f(x)}}\right\rvert\leq Ce^{C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, the following convergence holds in terms of moments:

L_{N}(f)\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m_{V}(f),\sigma^{2}(f)}}\right)

(1.9)

where the mean and the variance of the limiting Gaussian distribution are given by:

\displaystyle m_{V}(f)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{1}{\beta}}}\right)\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi^{-1}[f]^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(x)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(f)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\dfrac{1}{\beta\pi^{2}}\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}}}\right)^{2}\cdot\dfrac{1-xy}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\sqrt{1-y^{2}}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y

where the operator
\Xi^{-1}
is given explicitly in Lemma
4.1
(taking
\alpha=1
for
p>2
and
\alpha=0
for
p=2
).

Remark 1.2
.

•

Theorem
1.1
is a special case of Theorem
6.2
when specializing the interpolating parameter
\alpha=1
for
p>2
and
\alpha=0
for
p=2
.
This result is of course already known when
p=2
.

•

The hypothesis on the growth of
f
in Theorem
1.1
will be used in Lemma
5.3
to replace
f
by a truncated version, while preserving the convergence of moments. However one can deduce from Theorem
1.1
the weak convergence of the linear statistics
L_{N}(f)
for any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
with arbitrary growth at infinity by truncating
f
outside of the support of the equilibrium measure.

•

The mean
m_{V}(f)
can be rewritten, by Lemma
6.1
,

m_{V}(f)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta}}}\right)\bigg(\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{r^{\prime}(x)}{r(x)}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{f(t)-f(x)}{t-x}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sqrt{1-t^{2}}}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\\
-\frac{f(1)+f(-1)}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\bigg).

(1.10)

where
r(x)
is defined in (
1.25
) with
\alpha=1
.

For the next theorem, we define the entropy of
\mu
, a probability measure absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, by

{\mathrm{Ent}}[\mu]\coloneqq-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)}{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

(1.11)

Theorem 1.3

(Next-order expansion of the free energy)
.

Let
p\geq 2
, then as
N\rightarrow\infty
,

\dfrac{1}{N^{2}\beta}\log Z_{N}=-\dfrac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log 2+\dfrac{3}{2p}}}\right)+\dfrac{\log N}{2N}+\dfrac{F^{\{-1\}}}{N}+o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{1}{N}}}\right)

(1.12)

with

\displaystyle F^{\{-1\}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq F_{G}^{\{-1\}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{1}{\beta}-\dfrac{1}{2}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathrm{Ent}}[\mu_{V}]-\log\pi+\dfrac{1}{2}}}\right),

\displaystyle F^{\{-1\}}_{G}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{1}{\beta}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\dfrac{\beta}{2}+\log 2}}\right)-\dfrac{1}{2\beta}-\dfrac{1}{4}+\dfrac{1}{\beta}\log\dfrac{2\pi}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta/2}}\right)}.

Remark 1.4
.

In
[
16
,
17
]
, the authors proved a full
1/N
expansion for the free energy in the case of an analytic potential
V
. Due to our singular setting, we are only able to access the subleading order term.

To state the local law, we introduce the Stieltjes transforms:

s_{N}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{N}(\lambda)}{\lambda-z}=\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\dfrac{1}{\lambda_{k}-z},\hskip 28.45274pts_{V}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(\lambda)}{\lambda-z},

(1.13)

which is a well defined expression as soon as
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
for
s_{N}(z)
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]
for
s_{V}(z)
.
The
N
-dependent function
s_{N}
is called the empirical Stieltjes transform and
s_{V}
is called the equilibrium Stieltjes transform.

Theorem 1.5

(Optimal local law)
.

Let
p\geq 2
.
There exist
C,\delta_{0}>0
such that the following holds, with
\mathcal{R}\coloneqq[-1-\delta_{0},1+\delta_{0}]+\mathrm{i}(0,\delta_{0}]
:
for any
N\geq 1
,
q\geq 1
and
z=x+\mathrm{i}y\in\mathcal{R}
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s_{N}(z)-s_{V}(z)}}\right|^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq^{64})^{q}}{(Ny)^{q}},

and, if moreover
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert\leq 1+y
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s_{N}(z)-s_{V}(z)}}\right|^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq)^{q/2}}{(Ny)^{q}}+\frac{(Cq^{32})^{q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z^{2}-1}}\right\rvert^{1/2})^{q}}.

Remark 1.6
.

•

As
\im{z}
goes to zero, the imaginary part of the empirical Stieltjes transform at
z
can be seen as a smooth approximation to the empirical measure in an interval centered at
\re(z)
of size
\sim\im{z}
.

•

If
p
is even, then
V
is analytic and this local law was already obtained in
[
19
]
, with better bounds.

•

A local law is an important tool providing strong concentration results: via the use of the Hellfer–Sjöstrand formula one can deduce bounds on linear statistics
L_{N}(f)
(see Section
3
) or rigidity estimates
(see e.g.
[
19
, Lemma 3.8]
).
A rigidity estimate states that, with overwhelming probability, for each
1\leq k\leq N
, the
k
-th smallest particle is at distance at most
a_{N}N^{-2/3}[k\wedge(N-k+1)]^{-1/3}
of the
k
-th
N
-quantile of
\mu_{V}
, where
a_{N}\to\infty
is the precision of the rigidity estimate.
Such a rigidity estimate was established for
V\in\mathcal{C}^{4}(\mathbb{R})
and
a_{N}=N^{\varepsilon}
for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
in
[
18
]
.
Following the argument in
[
19
, Lemma 3.8]
, Theorem
1.5
yields a rigidity estimate with
a_{N}=(\log N)^{\ell}
for
\ell
large enough.

•

This local law is called optimal in the sense that it proves
s_{N}(z)-s_{V}(z)=O(1/(Ny))
, with a numerator which does not include a factor diverging as
N\to\infty
. We do not claim that the dependence of the numerator on
q
is optimal, and do not try to optimize it in the proof. Some improvements in this direction could be made, but to the cost of additional technicalities that we choose to avoid.

1.2.2
About the KLS conjecture for
p
-Schatten balls

As briefly mentioned above, the CLT for the
\beta
-ensembles allows us to give a consistency check on the Kannan–Lovász–Simonovits (KLS) conjecture for the
p
-Schatten balls,
p\geq 2
, that we describe below. Furthermore, the expansion for
\log Z_{N}
obtained in Theorem
1.3
implies an asymptotic expansion for the log-volume
\log|B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})|
of the
p
-Schatten balls that we describe below.

In convex geometry, and more specifically in the context of the study of high-dimensional convex bodies
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, this conjecture has been open for three decades
[
37
]
and takes its roots from the study of high-dimensional sampling algorithm, see
[
2
,
45
]
for a review. The conjecture posits that:

Conjecture 1.7

(KLS conjecture)
.

There exists a universal constant
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{univ}}>0
, such that for all
d\geq 1
,
f
smooth on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu
an absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and log-concave probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\Var_{\mu}f\leq\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{univ}}\cdot\lambda_{\mu}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\nabla f\|^{2}_{2}}}\right]

(1.14)

where
\lambda_{\mu}=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Var_{\mu}[\braket{\theta,X}]=\|\Cov(\mu)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
denotes the largest eigenvalue of the covariance matrix of
\mu
, given by
\Cov(\mu)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x_{i}x_{j}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x_{i}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x_{j}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
.

The KLS conjecture implies the variance (or thin-shell) conjecture (
i.e.
the previous conjecture in the special case
f(X)=\|X\|_{{\mathrm{HS}}}^{2}
) and the hyperplane conjecture (or Bourgain’s slicing problem). These two conjectures have been studied a lot and finally established in the recent papers
[
39
,
40
]
.

The thin-shell conjecture takes its roots in the papers
[
5
,
15
]
where the authors studied the rate of convergence of convex bodies around the normal distribution, known as the central limit problem for isotropic convex bodies. It can also be shown that a log-concave probability measure
\mu
satisfying the variance conjecture satisfies a strong concentration inequality, namely:

\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\|x\|-(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|X\|^{2})^{1/2}}}\right\rvert>t(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|X\|^{2})^{1/2}}}\right\}}}\right)\leq 2\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-C\sqrt{t}\frac{(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|X\|^{2})^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\mu}}}}}\right)

for a constant
C>0
and for all
t>0
.

Even though, the KLS conjecture is not known in general, it has been established in certain cases,
e.g.
for the uniform measures on the simplex and
\ell_{N}^{p}
balls, see
[
6
]
but also on the so-called Orlicz balls
[
41
,
7
]
defined as

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\sum_{i=1}^{p}f_{i}(\|x\|)\leq 1}}\right\}

where the
f_{i}
’s are called Young functions,
i.e.

f\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
increasing convex functions verifying
f(0)=0
. Our goal here is to study the case of
p
-Schatten balls in the spirit of
[
24
]
.

Let
\mathbb{F}\coloneqq\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{H}
and
M\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{F})
be a
N\times N
matrix with entries in
\mathbb{F}
. Denote its singular values by
(s_{1}(M),\dots,s_{N}(M))
,
i.e.
the eigenvalues of
\sqrt{M^{*}M}
. We define its
p
-Schatten norm for
p\in[1,\infty)
, denoted
\sigma_{p}(M)
by

\sigma_{p}(M)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}(M)^{p}}}\right)^{1/p}.

(1.15)

On the space
E=\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{F})_{{\mathrm{s.a.}}}
of self-adjoint matrices,
\sigma_{p}(M)
reduces to the
\ell^{p}
norm of the eigenvalues.
The couple
S_{p}^{N}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(E,\sigma_{p}}}\right)
defines a normed-vector space and we denote by
B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})
its unit ball

B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{M\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{F})_{{\mathrm{s.a.}}},\sigma_{p}(M)\leq 1}}\right\}.

Note that when
p=2
,
\sigma_{2}(M)=\|M\|_{{\mathrm{HS}}}\coloneqq\sqrt{{\mathrm{tr}}(M^{*}M)}
and we recover the Euclidean structure.

A link between the
\beta
-ensembles and the unit balls for the
p
-Schatten norms exists and is encoded in the following lemma.

Lemma 1.8

(Weyl’s integration formula,
[
54
,
29
]
)
.

For any
p\geq 1
and any
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
symmetric and positively homogeneous of degree
k
, we have:

\int_{B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})}F\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s_{1}(M),\dots,s_{N}(M)}}\right)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}M\\
=\dfrac{c_{N}}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{d_{N}+k}{p}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{N\beta}{2}c_{p}}}\right)^{\frac{d_{N}+k}{p}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\prod_{i<j}^{N}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{\beta}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-\frac{N\beta}{2}c_{p}|x_{i}|^{p}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{i},

where
c_{p}
is defined in (
1.5
),
c_{N}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathcal{U}_{N}(\mathbb{F})}}\right\rvert/(N!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathcal{U}_{1}(\mathbb{F})}}\right\rvert^{N})
with
\mathcal{U}_{N}(\mathbb{F})
the unitary group over
\mathbb{F}
,
\beta=1,2,4
according to
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
, and
d_{N}\coloneqq\frac{\beta}{2}N(N-1)+N
.

As an application with
F\equiv 1
, one gets the following relationship between the partition function
Z_{N}
introduced in (
1.2
) and the volume of
B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})
:

\lvert B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})\rvert=\dfrac{c_{N}}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{d_{N}}{p}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{N\beta}{2}c_{p}}}\right)^{\frac{d_{N}}{p}}Z_{N}.

(1.16)

Hence, the following asymptotic expansion of
\log\lvert B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})\rvert
, whose proof is given in Section
8.2
, follows directly from Theorem
1.3
.
The volume of Schatten balls, both in
\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{F})
and in
\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{F})_{{\mathrm{s.a.}}}
, was first studied in
[
54
]
which identified the first two orders of the expansion below, without identifying explicitly the constant of the 2nd order (except for
p=2
). This was done more recently in
[
36
,
35
]
, and recovered in
[
24
]
. We identify here the 3rd and 4th order.

Corollary 1.9

(Volume of
p
-Schatten balls)
.

As
N\xrightarrow{}\infty
,
\beta=1,2,4
,
p\geq 2
it holds that:

\log\lvert B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})\rvert=aN^{2}\log N+bN^{2}+cN\log N+dN+o(N),

where

a=-\frac{\beta}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{2}}}\right),\quad b=\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(pc_{p})}{2p}+\frac{1}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3}{2}+\log\frac{\pi}{\beta}}}\right)-\frac{1}{4p}}}\right),\quad c=\frac{\beta-2}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{2}}}\right),

d=\frac{2-\beta}{2p}\log(pc_{p})+\beta F^{\{-1\}}+\log\Gamma(\beta/2)+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\log(\pi\beta)}}\right)-\dfrac{1}{2}\log\frac{4\pi}{\beta},

where
c_{p}
is given by (
1.5
) and
F^{\{-1\}}
by Theorem
1.3
.

Note that the previous expansion allows for a more precise asymptotic expansion of the
q
-inertia moments in the spirit of
[
24
, Theorem 1.1]
namely to catch the
1/N
correction.

Besides the consideration on
\beta
-ensembles with Freud weights, another goal of the present paper was initially to establish the variance conjecture for the families of uniform laws on
B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})
for
N
large enough and
p\in(2,3]
which is implied by Theorem
1.1
. While the conjecture is now known, our result still allows us to give a consistency check of the KLS conjecture in the context of
p
-Schatten balls and for a specific class of
f
.

Theorem 1.10
.

For all
p\geq 2
,
r>0
an even integer and
q\in[1,\infty)
, the KLS conjecture, see Conjecture
1.7
, holds for
\mu
uniform laws on
B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})
with
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
,
N
large enough (depending on
p,q,r
) and
f(X)={\mathrm{Tr}}(X^{r})^{q}
with constant
4
,
i.e.

\Var_{\mu}f\leq 4\cdot\lambda_{\mu}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\nabla f\|^{2}_{2}}}\right].

1.3
Connection with the literature

Central limit theorems in
\beta
-ensembles.

The study of the fluctuations of the
\beta
-ensembles was initiated in the seminal paper
[
34
]
for polynomial
V
. These results were later extended to the case of analytic
V
in the one cut regime for
f
analytic
[
16
]
(see also
[
51
]
for a partial result) and in the multi-cut setting for
f
analytic
[
17
]
and for for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{6}

[
55
]
.
The regularity assumptions on
V
and
f
were relaxed in
[
9
]
, which covers the case
V\in\mathcal{C}^{6}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{4}
in the multi-cut setting, relying on the master operator method which is one of the two main ingredients in this paper.
They also cover some critical cases for a restricted class of test functions.
The currently best-known CLT in terms of
f
, requiring
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
, has been established for Wigner matrices in
[
44
]
.
Quantitative CLTs were obtained, via Stein’s method, for
V\in\mathcal{C}^{8}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{9}
in
[
43
]
, and for
V\in\mathcal{C}^{7}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1,\varepsilon}

[
4
]
.
In
[
19
]
, a CLT is proved with analytic
V
for singular test functions of the type
f(x)=\mathbbm{1}_{x\leq x_{0}}
or
f(x)=\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x-x_{0}}}\right\rvert
, which require an additional renormalization compared to previously mentioned cases.
A key tool in their paper is an optimal local law proved via loop equations: this method is our second main ingredient here.
CLTs at mesoscopic scales have also been developed, notably in
[
10
,
52
]
. Additionally, the high-temperature regime (
\beta=\beta_{N}\rightarrow 0
) has been studied in
[
13
,
49
,
32
,
27
,
46
]
.

Transition for bulk statistics at
p=1
.

For
\beta
-ensembles with Freud weights, it has been shown that the local behavior of the particles
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
under the distribution
\mathbb{P}_{N}
undergoes a transition at
p=1
, at least in the case
\beta=2

[
22
]
. This confirms earlier conjectures
[
21
]
, suggesting a dichotomy in the local statistics.
When
p>1
called the
strong confinement
regime, the equilibrium density (
1.6
) is still bounded and the local statistics are described asymptotically by the Sine-
\beta
process. In contrast, for
p<1
called the
weak confinement
regime, the density diverges at the origin and the local statistics are governed by a different, non-universal kernel.
Finally, in the critical case
p=1
, the density diverges logarithmically at the origin but surprisingly the local limit is still the Sine-
\beta
process.

Multicriticality and quantum chromodynamics.

The study of random matrix models at criticality,
i.e.
when the equilibrium density vanishes in the bulk, has attracted considerable interest in quantum field theories, see
[
25
,
58
]
. Owing to universality, understanding the microscopic behaviour near such critical points can lead to several predictions regarding some features of the spectrum of the Dirac operator in quantum chromodynamics in the critical universality class. It is well known that polynomial potentials
V_{\mathrm{pol}}
can generate critical
\beta
-ensembles in which the equilibrium density vanishes at the origin with an even integer exponent
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{2m}
with
m
an integer, see
e.g.

[
14
,
23
,
9
]
. However, this construction only yields polynomial vanishing with integer powers. To obtain a richer family of critical exponents, specifically real non-integer powers, a mixture of Freud weight and polynomial potentials was proposed in
[
1
,
33
]
. Indeed taking
V(x)=a\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p}-bx^{2}
for
2<p<3
and constants
a,b
finely tuned, the equilibrium density vanishes as
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-1}
thus exhibiting a real exponent. By further adding polynomial terms and increasing the power of the Freud weight, one can in fact generate arbitrary exponent.

1.4
Definitions and strategy

Our goal is twofold. We want to obtain the CLT and the subleading terms in the asymptotic expansion of
\log Z_{N}
.
Because of Lemma
7.2
, by working with the general potential
V_{\alpha}
defined by linearly interpolating between
V
and the quadratic potential as follows, for
p>2
,

V_{\alpha}(x)\coloneqq\alpha c_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p}+(1-\alpha)2x^{2},

(1.17)

and denoting

\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})\coloneqq\frac{1}{Z_{N,\alpha}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}}\right\rvert^{\beta}\cdot e^{-\frac{\beta N}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{N},

(1.18)

we can obtain the two results at once. Indeed, obtaining the CLT under
V_{\alpha}
for general
\alpha\in[0,1]
(in terms of the convergence of moments) implies Theorem
1.1
by taking
\alpha=1
; and one obtains Theorem
1.3
by integrating over
\alpha
the limits of
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[L_{N}(\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha})}}\right]
and using the asymptotic for the Selberg integral for
\alpha=1
, see Section
7
. Prior to explaining the strategy, we give the definitions that are going to be the main tools of our analysis.

The equilibrium measure.

We denote by
\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
the equilibrium measure under
V_{\alpha}
(recall it is defined as the unique minimizer of (
1.4
) with
V_{\alpha}
instead of
V
).
By
[
53
, Theorems I.1.5 and I.3.3]
, this measure is characterized by the existence of a constant
C_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the associated
effective potential
is nonnegative outside of the support:

V_{{\mathrm{eff}},\alpha}(x)\coloneqq\dfrac{V_{\alpha}(x)}{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x-y}}\right\rvert\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(y)-C_{\alpha}\begin{cases}=0&\text{on }\supp\mu_{V_{\alpha}},\\
\geq 0&\text{elsewhere}.\end{cases}

(1.19)

Recall that, for
\alpha=1
,
\mu_{V_{1}}=\mu_{V}
is given in (
1.6
). On the other hand, for
\alpha=0
, the equilibrium measure is the semi-circular distribution on
[-1,1]
.
Since they have the same support, it follows from the characterization above that
\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
is the linear interpolation between these two measures: hence, it is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, supported on
[-1,1]
and its density is given for all
x\in[-1,1]
by:

\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}}{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}(x)\coloneqq\alpha\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}}{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}(x)+\dfrac{2(1-\alpha)}{\pi}\sigma(x),

(1.20)

where we defined, for
x\in[-1,1]
,

\sigma(x)\coloneqq\sqrt{1-x^{2}}.

(1.21)

We now gather some basic facts on
\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
.
Differentiating the relationship (
1.19
) on
[-1,1]
, one gets the following formula (see e.g.
[
51
, Theorem 11.2.1]
)

\frac{V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)}{2}-\fint_{-1}^{1}\frac{1}{x-y}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(y)=0,

(1.22)

where
\fint
denotes the Cauchy principal value of the integral defined in (
1.30
).
Integrating this equality w.r.t.
h(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)
for some Hölder continuous function
h\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and symmetrizing the second term, one can deduce the following integral identity that we will use several times:

\int_{-1}^{1}V^{\prime}_{\alpha}(x)h(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)-\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\dfrac{h(x)-h(y)}{x-y}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)=0.

(1.23)

Another expression for the equilibrium measure is the following (see
[
51
, Theorem 11.2.4]
)

\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}}{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}(x)=\dfrac{\sigma(x)r_{\alpha}(x)}{\pi}\mathbbm{1}_{[-1,1]}(x),

(1.24)

where, for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we set

r_{\alpha}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)-V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)}{t-x}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}.

(1.25)

From the formula (
1.6
) for the equilibrium measure at
\alpha=1
, and expanding
(1-y^{2})^{-1/2}
, one gets for
x\in[-1,1]
the expression, valid for all
p>0

r_{\alpha}(x)=\frac{\alpha p}{\sigma(x)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p-1}\big(A_{p}-B_{p}\log|x|\big)-\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}a_{n}(p)x^{2n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{2n+1\neq p\}}}}\right)+2(1-\alpha),

(1.26)

where

A_{p}\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathbbm{1}_{\{2n+1\neq p\}}\frac{\Gamma(n+1/2)}{n!(2n+1-p)\Gamma(1/2)},\qquad B_{p}\coloneqq\frac{\Gamma(p/2)}{((p-1)/2)!\Gamma(1/2)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{p\in 2\mathbb{N}+1\}},

a_{n}(p)=\frac{\Gamma(n+1/2)}{n!(2n-p+1)\Gamma(1/2)}.

Finally, we emphasize that
\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
is symmetric w.r.t. 0 because
V_{\alpha}
is even (see (
1.20
).

Strategy.

We use the master operator approach already used to prove CLTs in

[
16
,
55
,
17
,
9
]
.
The
master operator

\Xi_{\alpha}
acts on functions
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and is defined as

\Xi_{\alpha}[\psi](x)\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}\psi(x)V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)+\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\psi(x)-\psi(t)}{x-t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(t),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.27)

This operator is invertible (see
[
8
, Lemma 3.2]
,
[
9
, Lemma 3.3]
and Lemma
4.1
here)
via
Tricomi’s formula
[
56
]
, and the understanding of the function
\psi_{\alpha}\coloneqq\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f]
and its derivatives (for
f
a smooth function) is of prime importance. Indeed, using a classic trick based on the change of variables

\lambda_{k}=y_{k}+\psi(y_{k})/N
for
1\leq k\leq N
in the partition function with respect to
V_{\alpha}
and a Taylor expansion, one can get the following estimate:

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L_{N}(f)+\dfrac{A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})}{N}+{\mathrm{error}}}}\right)}}\right]=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m_{V_{\alpha}}(f)+\dfrac{\sigma^{2}(f)}{2}}}\right).

(1.28)

Above

m_{V_{\alpha}}(f)
and
\sigma^{2}(f)
are respectively the mean and the variance of the limiting Gaussian distribution defined in Theorem
6.2
, the
anisotropy
term
A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})
is given by

A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})=N^{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)-\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime})}{\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}(\mu_{N}-\mu_{V_{\alpha}})(\lambda)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}(\mu_{N}-\mu_{V_{\alpha}})(\lambda^{\prime}),

and the error term can be showed to be negligible by applying the law of large numbers,
i.e.
the convergence of
\mu_{N}
towards
\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
.
Thus, the following convergence in terms of moments holds

L_{N}(f)+\dfrac{A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})}{N}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m_{V_{\alpha}}(f),\sigma^{2}(f)}}\right).

(1.29)

Therefore, proving that
A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})/N
converges to 0 in terms of moments is enough to conclude on the CLT.

Proofs of CLTs via this method usually start with some
a priori
concentration bounds obtained by studying the energy functional in (
1.4
), see
e.g.

[
9
, Corollary 2.5]
or
[
31
, Corollary 4.16]
that we use here. This bounds show that
L_{N}(f)=O(\sqrt{N})
and therefore
A_{N}(f)=O(N)
for smooth enough
f
(with sometimes additional logarithmic factors that we neglect in this discussion), and therefore are not enough to conclude.
A one-step bootstrap is necessary: with this input in hand, coming back to (
1.28
) shows that
L_{N}(f)=O(1)
and so
A_{N}(f)=O(1)
, which is enough to conclude that
A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})/N
converges to 0. The improved concentration bounds and then the CLT usually require more and more regularity on the test function
f
, because the function
\psi_{\alpha}=\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f]
is less regular than
f
.

In our case, the main difficulty is that, if
p\in(2,3)
, the function
\psi_{\alpha}
is only
\mathcal{C}^{2}
, with
\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)
exploding as
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p-3}
at the origin, even for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
because this singularity is due to the singularity at 0 of the density of the equilibrium measure. This does not provide enough regularity even for applying the
a priori
bound on
A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})
, which requires
\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}
bounded in
[
31
, Corollary 4.16]
and could be improved to require only
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rVert_{2}<\infty
which does not hold either if
p\leq 5/2
.
Hence, we need a way to reduce the regularity needed for our concentration bounds: this is done by proving local laws, namely an estimate on the moments of
s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
where we recall (
1.13
).
Indeed, if we know that
s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)=O(M/Ny)
for some
M
depending on
N
and
z=x+\mathrm{i}y
, then it follows from Helffer–Sjöstrand formula that
A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})=O(M^{2})
provided
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rVert_{1}<\infty
, see Lemma
3.4
and Remark
3.5
.

The proof of local laws relies on loop equations, which are relationships between moments of linear statistics obtained by integration by part.
In
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]
, it has been observed that these loop equations can be combined in a way which is almost self-contained in terms of
s_{N}(z)
, up to a linear statistics
L_{N}(\varphi_{z})
where
\varphi_{z}(\lambda)=(V^{\prime}(\lambda)-V^{\prime}(z))/(\lambda-z)
(note that this behaves well when
z
approaches the axis, unlike the Stieltjes transform).
Their argument has two independent parts: first, using a contour integral trick which works if
V
is analytic, they show that
L_{N}(\varphi_{z})=O(1)
, then they analyze the rest of the loop equation to prove that
s_{N}(z)
almost solves the same quadratic equation as
s_{V}(z)
and therefore they are close from each other.
The second part does not rely heavily on analyticity of
V
and can be adapted to our context.
An argument similar to the first part has already been applied to a potential
V\in\mathcal{C}^{4}
in
[
18
, Lemma 6.6]
by proceeding by induction on the scales to obtain a (sub-optimal) local law, but it does not seem easy to adapt this approach to our less regular
V
.
Instead, we simply replace the first part of the argument by bounds on
L_{N}(h_{z})
obtained by other means:
a priori
bounds first, and then bounds obtained via the master operator approach.
To summarize, this
local law machinery
provides a tool which improves concentration bound, by lowering the regularity required for the test function, without improving the dependence on
N
.

One subtlety in the local law argument that we would like to emphasize is that the function
\varphi_{z}
mentioned above is also not regular enough to apply the
a priori
bounds or the master operator approach to
L_{N}(\varphi_{z})
.
To overcome this issue, we modify the loop equations in order to replace
\varphi_{z}
by a function
f_{z}
with an additional level of regularity, in exchange of a singular
1/z
factor, see Remark
2.4
for details. This argument is crucial to be able to cover the case
p\in(2,3]
and the bootstrap argument would not work in that case without this regularization of the local law.

Overall, the local law machinery is applied successively five times: a first time to obtain a local law from the
a priori
bounds (Corollary
5.5
) and then four times in the proof of the optimal local law in Section
5.3
.
Between each application, the bound on
L_{N}(f_{z})
is improved, either by applying the master operator approach and using the previous local law to bound
A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})
to improve the dependence on
N
, or by applying the local law directly to bound
L_{N}(f_{z})
to improve the dependence on
z
.

1.5
Organization of the paper

In Section
2
, we state the loop equations and prove that from a certain concentration estimate, we can deduce a local law. We prove in Section
3
that by using the Helffer–Sjöstrand formula, a local law translates into estimates on the moments of linear statistics and of the anisotropy. In Section
4
, we state several bounds on some functions of interest and on a certain regularization of the test-functions considered. Section
5
is dedicated to establishing the first concentration bound from which we deduce a first local law. We then improve this local law iteratively, by establishing better and better concentration bounds, until obtaining an optimal local law. Finally, we prove respectively the CLT (Theorem
1.1
), the third order asymptotic expansion for the free energy (Theorem
1.3
) and the check on the KLS conjecture (Theorem
1.10
) and the computation of the volume of the Schatten balls (Corollary
1.9
) in Sections
6
,
7
and
8
. We gather in Appendix
A
the proofs of the estimates stated in Section
4
, and in Appendix
B
the proof of the stability lemma (Lemma
2.6
), which is a key ingredient for proving the local laws of Section
2
.

1.6
Notation

Throughout the paper,
C
and
c
denote positive constants that can change between occurrences. They can depend on some other parameters in a way which is made clear in the statement of each result; in the proof of such a result allowed dependencies are the same as in the statement.

We always consider the principal branch of the logarithm, extended to the negative real numbers by continuity from above, that is
\log(re^{\mathrm{i}\theta})=\log(r)+\mathrm{i}\theta
for any
r>0
and
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi]
. We also consider the associated square root
\sqrt{z}=\exp(\frac{1}{2}\log z)
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
with continuous extension at 0.
For
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with a singularity at
x_{0}
, we write

\fint_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{-\infty}^{x_{0}-\varepsilon}f(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x+\int_{x_{0}+\varepsilon}^{\infty}f(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}}\right)

(1.30)

for the Cauchy principal value, whenever the previous limit makes sense.

We write
\mathbb{R}^{*}=\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and, for
a\leq b
integers,
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket=\{a,a+1,\dots,b\}
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
x_{+}=x\vee 0
.
For
E\subset\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{k}(E)
the set of class
\mathcal{C}^{k}
real-valued functions
f
on
E
and write
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{k}(E)}\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{k}\sup_{x\in E}\lvert f^{(i)}(x)\rvert
, which is not a norm if
E
is non compact because then it can be infinite.
We simply write
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{k}}
if
E=\mathbb{R}
.
Finally, we write
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
for the set of functions in
\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R})
with compact support.

2
Loop equations and local laws

2.1
Loop equations

Before stating the loop equations, we need to define some functions that are going to be central in our analysis.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
p>2
.
We define the function
g
as follows:

\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\quad g(x)\coloneqq pc_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p}=xV^{\prime}(x).

(2.1)

We extend it pseudo-analytically on
\mathbb{C}
as follows:

\forall z=x+\mathrm{i}y\in\mathbb{C},\quad g(z)\coloneqq g(x)+\mathrm{i}yg^{\prime}(x)-\frac{y^{2}}{2}g^{\prime\prime}(x)-\mathrm{i}\frac{y^{3}}{6}g^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\chi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{y}{x}}}\right),

(2.2)

where
\chi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is an even function such that
\mathbbm{1}_{[-1/2,1/2]}\leq\chi\leq\mathbbm{1}_{[-1,1]}
and where the last term is meant to be 0 when
x=0
.
We also define for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the functions

g_{\alpha}(z)\coloneqq\alpha g(z)+4(1-\alpha)z^{2}

(2.3)

and
f_{\alpha,z}
by setting:

\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\quad f_{\alpha,z}(\lambda)\coloneqq\frac{g_{\alpha}(\lambda)-g_{\alpha}(z)}{\lambda-z}.

(2.4)

Finally, we define the random variable
P_{\alpha}(z)
for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
by:

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq s_{N}(z)^{2}+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}s_{N}(z)+\frac{h_{\alpha}(z)}{z},

(2.5)

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{-1}^{1}f_{\alpha,z}(t)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(t),

(2.6)

where
s_{N}
is defined in (
1.13
) and
\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
is defined in (
1.20
).

Remark 2.2
.

Note that the pseudo-analytic extension of
g
is well-defined since
p>2
implies that
g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
.
The truncation involving the function
\chi
is needed for the last term because
g^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)
diverges at 0 when
p\in(2,3)
. This definition makes
g
a continuous function on
\mathbb{C}
; indeed, to prove the continuity at 0, one can note that, for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}
, there exists
C>0
such that, for any
z\in K
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert y^{3}g^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\chi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{y}{x}}}\right)}}\right\rvert\leq c_{p}p^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert y}}\right\rvert^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p-3}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert y}}\right\rvert\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert y}}\right\rvert^{p\wedge 3},

(2.7)

using
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p-3}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert y}}\right\rvert^{p-3}
if
p<3
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p-3}\leq C
if
p\geq 3
, and the RHS of (
2.7
) vanishes as
z\to 0
.

We are now ready to state the loop equations that are the main ingredient to derive the local laws. Denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}
the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
, where we recall (
1.18
).

Proposition 2.3

(Loop equations)
.

Let
p>2
. For all
N,q\geq 1
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
,
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(P_{\alpha}(z)+\frac{L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}{Nz}+\frac{1}{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\beta}-1}}\right)s_{N}^{\prime}(z)}}\right)P_{\alpha}(z)^{q-1}P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})^{q}}}\right]

\displaystyle{}+\frac{2(q-1)}{\beta N^{2}z}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2s_{N}(z)+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\lambda_{k}-z)^{3}}}}\right)P_{\alpha}(z)^{q-2}P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})^{q}}}\right]

\displaystyle{}+\frac{2q}{\beta N^{2}z}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2s_{N}(\overline{z})+\frac{g_{\alpha}(\overline{z})}{\overline{z}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\lambda_{k}-\overline{z})^{2}(\lambda_{k}-z)}}}\right)P_{\alpha}(z)^{q-1}P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})^{q-1}}}\right]=0.

(2.8)

Proof.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
G\colon\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be
\mathcal{C}^{1}
functions with at most polynomial growth at infinity.
We write
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})
. Integrating by parts the factor
\partial_{\lambda_{k}}(f(\lambda_{k})G(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta N^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f^{\prime}(\lambda_{k})G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})+f(\lambda_{k})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\beta N^{2}}\frac{1}{Z_{N,\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f(\lambda_{k})G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}\right)\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}}\right\rvert-\frac{\beta N}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})}}\right)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{N}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}f(\lambda_{k})V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{k})-\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{k\neq\ell}\frac{f(\lambda_{k})-f(\lambda_{\ell})}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{\ell}}}}\right)G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}\right],

where the second term has been symmetrized noting that
\sum_{k\neq\ell}\frac{f(\lambda_{k})}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{\ell}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\neq\ell}\frac{f(\lambda_{k})-f(\lambda_{\ell})}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{\ell}}
.
Applying this to
f(\lambda)=1/(\lambda-z)
, we get, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s_{N}(z)^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{k})}{\lambda_{k}-z}+\frac{1}{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\beta}-1}}\right)s^{\prime}_{N}(z)}}\right)G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}\right]=\frac{2}{\beta N^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{k}}G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}{\lambda_{k}-z}}}\right].

(2.9)

On the other hand, with
f(\lambda)=1
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{k})}}\right)G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}\right]=\frac{2}{\beta N^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{k=1}^{N}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}\right].

(2.10)

Summing (
2.9
) with (
2.10
) divided by
z
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s_{N}(z)^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{k})}{z(\lambda_{k}-z)}+\frac{1}{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\beta}-1}}\right)s_{N}^{\prime}(z)}}\right)G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}\right]=\frac{2}{\beta N^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}{z(\lambda_{k}-z)}}}\right].

(2.11)

Recalling the definition of
P_{\alpha}
and
f_{\alpha,z}
in Definition
2.1
and of
L_{N}
in (
1.7
), this can be then rewritten as

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(P_{\alpha}(z)+\frac{L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}{Nz}+\frac{1}{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\beta}-1}}\right)s_{N}^{\prime}(z)}}\right)G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}\right]=\frac{2}{\beta N^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}{z(\lambda_{k}-z)}}}\right].

(2.12)

Finally, we choose
G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=P_{\alpha}(z)^{q-1}P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})^{q}
, and a direct computation on the sum on the right-hand side yields

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}[P_{\alpha}(z)^{q-1}P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})^{q}]}{z(\lambda_{k}-z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(q-1)}{z}P_{\alpha}(z)^{q-2}P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-2s_{N}(z)-\dfrac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right]\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\lambda_{k}-z)^{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{q}{z}P_{\alpha}(z)^{q-1}P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})^{q-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-2s_{N}(\overline{z})-\dfrac{g_{\alpha}(\overline{z})}{\overline{z}}}}\right]\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\lambda_{k}-\overline{z})^{2}(\lambda_{k}-z)},

and the result follows.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

The loop equations used in
[
19
]
to prove their local law are the one obtained by taking
f(\lambda)=1/(\lambda-z)
as in (
2.9
) and
G(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=P_{\alpha}(z)^{q-1}P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})^{q}
.
Therefore, they differ from the loop equation above in (
2.8
) by the fact that
L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})/(Nz)
is replaced by

\frac{1}{N}L_{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda\mapsto\frac{V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda)-V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)}{\lambda-z}}}\right),

where
V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)
naturally makes sense in their context because the potential is analytic, as well as other differences in the 2nd and 3rd line of (
2.8
) which are not significant for the analysis.
Hence, the fact of combining the loop equations (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) to get (
2.11
) allows us to change the test function appearing in the linear statistics, in order to gain regularity (
g_{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
whereas
V_{\alpha}^{\prime}
is only
\mathcal{C}^{1}
) in exchange of a singular
1/z
factor.
This idea, which plays a key role in our proof, is inspired by an argument of a similar taste used in
[
28
]
in the context of Laguerre ensembles (see how (3.3) and (3.4) are combined into (3.8) there).

2.2
Local laws

In this section, our goal is to prove Proposition (
2.7
), which turns a bound on moments of
L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})
into a local law.
To this end, we adapt to our setting the arguments of
[
19
]
that lead to their local law.

Before stating the local law, we need the following lemmas on the equilibrium Stieltjes transform
s_{V_{\alpha}}
, whose proofs can be found in Appendix
B
. We recall that
s_{N}
and
s_{V_{\alpha}}
are defined in (
1.13
).

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
p>2
.

(i)

For all
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]
,
s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
solves the quadratic equation

X^{2}+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}X+\frac{h_{\alpha}(z)}{z}=0,

(2.13)

where
g_{\alpha}
and
h_{\alpha}
are defined in Definition
2.1
.

(ii)

Define for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
,

r_{\alpha}(z)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi z}\int_{-1}^{1}\dfrac{g_{\alpha}(t)-g_{\alpha}(z)}{t-z}\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}.

(2.14)

This definition is consistent with the previous definition of
r_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{R}
given in (
1.25
).

(iii)

For all
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]
,

s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)=r_{\alpha}(z)b(z)-\dfrac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{2z},

(2.15)

where
b(z)\coloneqq\sqrt{z+1}\sqrt{z-1}
.

(iv)

For all
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]
, the other solution of (
2.13
) is given by

\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)\coloneqq-r_{\alpha}(z)b(z)-\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{2z}.

(2.16)

Relying on the fact that
s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
and
\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
are the roots of (
2.13
), we can state the following lemma which is crucial to derive the local laws. This lemma states, among other technical results, that: for all
z
in a rectangular region
\mathcal{R}
above an enlargement of the spectrum, whenever
u\in\mathbb{C}
almost solves (
2.13
), then
u
is close to either
s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
or
\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
.
Moreover when
z
is in a trapezoid region
\mathcal{T}
above the spectrum and
u
is such that
\im(u)>0
, then we can argue
u
is indeed close to
s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
, using that
\im(s_{V_{\alpha}}(z))>0
whereas
\im(\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z))<0
.
This result is reminiscent of various results in random matrix theory, and is in particular the analogue of
[
19
, Lemma B.1]
proven in the case where
V
is analytic. The proof for the singular potential
V_{\alpha}
requires some additional steps and can be found in Appendix
B.2
.

Lemma 2.6

(Stability lemma)
.

There exist
\delta_{0},C>0
depending only on
p>2
such that the following bounds hold true for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
Let

\mathcal{R}\coloneqq[-1-\delta_{0},1+\delta_{0}]+\mathrm{i}(0,\delta_{0}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\{x+\mathrm{i}y\in\mathcal{R}:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert\leq 1+y\}.

(2.17)

Let
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
,
z\in\mathcal{R}
and
u\in\mathbb{C}
such that

u^{2}+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}u+\frac{h_{\alpha}(z)}{z}=\zeta.

Then, recalling (
2.16
),

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert u-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert u-\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\zeta}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\zeta}}\right\rvert^{1/2}}}\right).

(2.18)

If
\im(u)>0
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im(u-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z))}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert u-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert u-\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert}}\right).

(2.19)

If
\im(u)>0
and
z\in\mathcal{T}
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert u-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\zeta}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\zeta}}\right\rvert^{1/2}}}\right).

(2.20)

We now have all the ingredients needed to state and prove the local law machinery via the loop equations and stability lemma.
This results establishes a local law provided that we have a bound on moments of the linear statistics
L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})
.

Proposition 2.7

(Local law machinery)
.

Let
p>2
.
Let
\delta_{0}
,
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{T}
be as in Lemma
2.6
.
Then there exists
C>0
such that the following holds, for any
N\geq 1
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
Assume that there exist
a\geq 1
and
B_{N}\colon\mathcal{R}\to(0,\infty)
such that, for any
z\in\mathcal{R}
and
q\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq(q^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}.

(2.21)

Then,

(i)

[Inside the trapezoid]
For any
z\in\mathcal{T}
and
q\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq)^{q/2}}{(Ny)^{q}}+\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z^{2}-1}}\right\rvert^{1/2})^{q}}.

(ii)

[Outside the trapezoid]

Let
B_{\max}\coloneqq 1\vee\sup_{z\in\overline{\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}}}B_{N}(z)
.
Then, for any
z=\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
and
q\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq)^{2aq}B_{\max}^{2q}}{(Ny)^{q}}.

Remark 2.8
.

•

In the proof below as in many other places in the paper, it is convenient to restrict ourselves to bound even moments.
This is enough because the odd case can then be deduced via the inequality
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert X}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert X}}\right\rvert^{2q}]^{1/2}
up to a change of the constant
C
in the bound.

•

We will start with the initial
B_{N}(z)=C\sqrt{N\log N}
given by Corollary
5.4
. The local law that we will deduce will allow us, through the use of the Helffer–Sjöstrand formula (see Section
3
) and the master operator
\Xi_{\alpha}
(see Section
4
) to obtain in Section
5
the better (in
N
but worse in
q
) estimate:

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})|^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{2}(\log N)^{2}\dfrac{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}}}\right)^{q}.

We will then use
B_{N}(z)=C(\log N)^{2}/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert
and
a=2
. We will then obtain a local law from which we will deduce the improved estimate:

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{4}(\log N)^{4}}}\right)^{q}.

Applying the same strategy we will get the further improved bound

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq(Cq^{32})^{q},

Allow us to obtain an optimal local law (in
N
) from which we will deduce Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.3
.

We first prove the local law inside the trapezoid region
\mathcal{T}
(recall (
2.17
)) using the same approach as
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]
. Recall that the constants
C>0
appearing in our bounds can depend on
\beta
and
p
, can be increased from occurrence to occurrence, but are always uniform in
N
,
q
,
z
and
\alpha
.

Proof of Proposition
2.7
.
(i)
.

We first work with
z\in\mathcal{R}
and restrict ourselves to
z\in\mathcal{T}
later in the proof.
First note that by definition of
g
in (
2.2
) and the fact that
\chi
is chosen to be an even function, we have
g(\overline{z})=\overline{g(z)}
and therefore
P_{\alpha}(\overline{z})=\overline{P_{\alpha}(z)}
.
Then, it follows from the loop equation in Proposition
2.3
together with the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q-1}}}\right]+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 1-2/\beta}}\right\rvert}{N}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}^{\prime}(z)}}\right\rvert\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q-1}}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\frac{4q-2}{\beta N^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2s_{N}(z)+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}-z}}\right\rvert^{3}}}}\right)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q-2}}}\right].

(2.22)

Recall Young’s inequality: if
a^{-1}+b^{-1}=1
, then
xy\leq\frac{x^{a}}{a}+\frac{y^{b}}{b}
for any
x,y\geq 0
.
We apply Young’s inequality to each term on the RHS of (
2.22
), by introducing a factor
\lambda>0
and
1/\lambda
, and taking
a=2q
and
b=2q/(2q-1)
for the first two terms, and
a=q
and
b=q/(q-1)
for the third term.
Thus, the RHS of (
2.22
) is at most

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\cdot\frac{2q-1}{2q\lambda^{\frac{2q}{2q-1}}}+\frac{q-1}{q\lambda^{\frac{q}{q-1}}}}}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]+\frac{\lambda^{2q}}{2q(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]+\frac{(\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 1-2/\beta}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}{2qN^{2q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}^{\prime}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]

\displaystyle{}+\frac{\lambda^{q}(4q-2)^{q}}{q(\beta N^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2s_{N}(z)+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert^{q}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}-z}}\right\rvert^{3}}}}\right)^{q}}}\right].

(2.23)

Now, we can choose
\lambda=6
so that

2\cdot\frac{2q-1}{2q\lambda^{\frac{2q}{2q-1}}}+\frac{q-1}{q\lambda^{\frac{q}{q-1}}}\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\lambda}=\frac{1}{2},

and subtracting the
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}]
term to the LHS of (
2.22
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C^{q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]+\frac{C^{q}}{N^{2q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}^{\prime}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(N^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2s_{N}(z)+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert^{q}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}-z}}\right\rvert^{3}}}}\right)^{q}}}\right].

(2.24)

Then, using the assumption (
2.21
) on
L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})
and the following bounds

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}^{\prime}(z)}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}-z}}\right\rvert^{2}}=\frac{\im s_{N}(z)}{y},

and, using
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}}}\right\rvert\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}-z}}\right\rvert+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert
,

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}-z}}\right\rvert^{3}}\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}-z}}\right\rvert^{2}}+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{k}-z}}\right\rvert^{3}}\leq\frac{\im s_{N}(z)}{y}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}{y}}}\right),

we obtain:

\hskip-4.26773pt\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im s_{N}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2s_{N}(z)+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert^{q}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im s_{N}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right].

(2.25)

We now restrict ourselves to
z\in\mathcal{T}
.
By (
2.20
), we know that, since
z\in\mathcal{T}
and
\im s_{N}(z)>0
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{1/2}}}\right).

(2.26)

We introduce

\Lambda(z)\coloneqq\im s_{N}(z)\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2s_{N}(z)+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert

and we bound

\Lambda(z)\leq 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\im s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert}}\right)\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{1/2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert}}\right),

where, for the 1st term, we used (
2.26
) and, for the 2nd term, we recall that
s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)=r_{\alpha}(z)b(z)-\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{2z}
(see Lemma
2.5
.
(i)
) and used (
B.13
) and (
B.12
).
Hence, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\Lambda(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C^{q}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]+C^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q/2}}{(Ny)^{q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\Lambda(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]^{1/2}+C^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q},

where we used
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}]^{1/2}
and then (
2.25
).
Using that
x\leq a\sqrt{x}+b\Rightarrow x\leq a^{2}+b
for
x,a,b>0
, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\Lambda(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]\leq C^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}+\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}.

(2.27)

Using this bound in (
2.25
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}\frac{(q^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{2q}}{(Ny)^{4q}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}+\frac{(Cq)^{2q}}{(Ny)^{4q}},

(2.28)

where we neglected one term by using
ab\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
. We now distinguish according to which term is the largest in the RHS of (
2.28
):

•

If the largest term is the first or second one, then, up to an increasing of
C
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q},

and, using the inequality
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{C}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert
coming from (
2.26
), we deduce

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert zb(z)}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}.

•

If the largest term is the third one, then we get

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]\leq\dfrac{C^{q}q^{2q}}{(Ny)^{4q}}.

Thus, by using
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right|\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right|^{1/2}
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert zb(z)}}\right\rvert)^{2q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}.

In all cases, we obtained the desired bound so this concludes the proof of Part
(i)
of Proposition
2.7
(recall from Remark
2.8
that it is enough to bound even moments).
∎

Before proving Part
(ii)
in Proposition
2.7
, we need the following result which is the analogue of
[
19
, Proposition 2.5]
. It consists in a bound on the imaginary part of the differences between the two Stieltjes transforms
s_{N}
and
s_{V_{\alpha}}
, which will be necessary to extend the local law
(i)
outside the trapezoid region.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
p>2
.
Let
\delta_{0}
,
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{T}
be as in Lemma
2.6
.
Then there exists
C,C^{\prime}>0
such that the following holds, for any
N\geq 1
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
Assume that there exist
a>0
and
B_{N}\colon\mathcal{R}\to(0,\infty)
such that, for any
z\in\mathcal{R}
and
q\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq(q^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}.

(2.29)

Then, for any
z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
and any
q\geq 1
, setting
\kappa\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x-1}}\right\rvert\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x+1}}\right\rvert
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im(s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z))}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\begin{cases}\dfrac{(Cq)^{q/2}}{N^{q}(y\kappa)^{q/2}}+\dfrac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}\kappa^{q/2}}+\dfrac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}}{N^{q}\kappa^{q/2}},&\text{if }y\geq\sqrt{C^{\prime}q}/(N\sqrt{\kappa}),\\
\dfrac{(Cq)^{q/2}}{(Ny)^{q}}+\dfrac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q/2}}{N^{q/2}},&\text{if }y\leq\sqrt{C^{\prime}q}/(N\sqrt{\kappa}).\end{cases}

The same bounds hold for the moments of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert
.

Proof.

Let
z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
.
We recall that
\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
, the other solution of (
2.13
), is given by (
2.16
).
It follows from (
2.25
) and the fact that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert
is bounded away from 0 for
z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
that

\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{N^{2q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im s_{N}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2s_{N}(z)+\frac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert^{q}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im s_{N}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right].

(2.30)

We now define
\Upsilon(z)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert
where
\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
is the other solution of
P_{\alpha}(z)
. From (
2.19
), a bound on moments of
\Upsilon(z)
will be enough to conclude. Using (
B.15
), we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im s_{N}(z)}}\right\rvert\leq\Upsilon(z)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im{s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert\leq\Upsilon(z)+\frac{Cy}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert}.

We also have by the expressions (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) for
s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
and
\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)
,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2s_{N}(z)+\dfrac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\Upsilon(z)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)+\dfrac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 2\widetilde{s}_{V_{\alpha}}(z)+\dfrac{g_{\alpha}(z)}{z}}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=2\Upsilon(z)+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\widetilde{r}_{\alpha}(z)b(z)}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\Upsilon(z)+C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert,

using in the last inequality that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\widetilde{r}_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert\leq C
by (
B.12
).
Plugging this in (
2.30
) and using the fact that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert\leq C\sqrt{\kappa}
and therefore
y^{q}/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}\leq C
(because
y\leq\kappa
), we obtain:

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{2q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{N^{2q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Upsilon(z)^{2q}+y^{q}+\kappa^{q/2}\Upsilon(z)^{q}}}\right].

(2.31)

On the other hand, by the stability lemma (see (
2.18
)) and the inequality
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert\leq C\sqrt{\kappa}
, we have

\Upsilon(z)\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert^{1/2}}}\right).

(2.32)

We now distinguish according to which term is the largest in the RHS of (
2.31
):

•

If the largest term is the second one, then
by following the arguments of the proof of
[
19
, Proposition 2.5]
(see after (2.44) in their proof), there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that, if
y\geq C^{\prime}\sqrt{q}/(N\sqrt{\kappa})
, by using
\Upsilon(z)\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P_{\alpha}(z)}}\right\rvert/\sqrt{\kappa}
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Upsilon(z)^{2q}}}\right]\leq\dfrac{(Cq)^{q}}{N^{2q}y^{q}\kappa^{q}}+\dfrac{(Cq)^{2q}}{(Ny)^{4q}\kappa^{q}}

and, if
y\leq C^{\prime}\sqrt{q}/(N\sqrt{\kappa})
, by using
\Upsilon(z)\leq C|P_{\alpha}(z)|^{1/2}
instead, we get

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Upsilon(z)^{2q}}}\right]\leq\dfrac{(Cq)^{q}}{(Ny)^{2q}}.

•

If the largest term is the first one, then by (
2.32
), we have

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Upsilon(z)^{2q}}}\right]\leq C^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\dfrac{(q^{a}B_{N}(z))^{2q}}{N^{2q}\kappa^{q}}\wedge\dfrac{(q^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}}{N^{q}}}}\right],

and we choose to use the first part of the bound when
y\geq C^{\prime}\sqrt{q}/(N\sqrt{\kappa})
and the second part otherwise.

Summing over these two cases yields the result.
∎

We also need the following lemma which is the analogue of
[
19
, Lemma 3.6]
. It is a bound on the moments of the number of outliers,
i.e.
the number of particles outside of the limiting spectrum
(-1,1)
. Let
I
be an interval, we define

\mathcal{N}(I)\coloneqq\#\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:\lambda_{i}\in I}}\right\},

(2.33)

which is the number of particles in
I
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
p>2
.
Let
\delta_{0}
,
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{T}
be as in Lemma
2.6
.
Then there exists
C>0
such that the following holds, for any
N\geq 1
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
Assume that there exist
a\geq 1
and
B_{N}\colon\mathcal{R}\to(0,\infty)
such that, for any
z\in\mathcal{R}
and
q\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq(q^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}.

(2.34)

Let
B_{\max}\coloneqq 1\vee\sup_{z\in\overline{\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}}}B_{N}(z)
.
Then, for any
q\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{N}([1,\infty))^{q}}}\right]\leq(Cq)^{2aq}B_{\max}^{2q}.

(2.35)

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
19
, Lemma 3.6]
, but we have to keep track of the role of the factor
(q^{a}B_{N}(z))^{q}
appearing in the bound of Lemma
2.9
and which is not present in
[
19
]
.

Preliminaries.
We first gather some tools for the proof.
First note that, for any
z=x+\mathrm{i}y
with
y>0
,

\mathcal{N}([x-y,x+y])=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda_{i}-x}}\right\rvert\leq y}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\dfrac{2y^{2}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z-\lambda_{i}}}\right\rvert^{2}}=2yN\cdot\im s_{N}(z).

(2.36)

On the other hand, by (
B.15
), there exists
C_{0}>0
such that for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
, we have
\im s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)\leq(C_{0}^{2}y)/(4\sqrt{\kappa})
, where we used
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert b(z)}}\right\rvert\geq\sqrt{\kappa}
with
\kappa\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x-1}}\right\rvert\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x+1}}\right\rvert
.
Therefore, for
z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
such that
y\leq\kappa^{1/4}/(C_{0}\sqrt{N})
, we have
\im s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)\leq 1/(4yN)
and, using that
\mathcal{N}(I)
is an integer, it follows from (
2.36
) that

\mathcal{N}([x-y,x+y])\leq 4yN\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\im(s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z))}}\right\rvert.

Combining this with Lemma
2.9
, there exists
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that, for any
q\geq 1
and
z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
such that
\sqrt{C_{1}q}/(N\sqrt{\kappa})\leq y\leq\kappa^{1/4}/(C_{0}\sqrt{N})
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{N}([x-y,x+y])^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{C_{2}qy}{\kappa}}}\right)^{q/2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{C_{2}q}{Ny\sqrt{\kappa}}}}\right)^{q}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{C_{2}q^{a}B_{\max}y}{\kappa}}}\right)^{q},

(2.37)

where we bounded
\sqrt{\kappa}\geq\kappa\delta_{0}^{-1/2}
in the denominator fo the last term.
Note that this inequality cannot be applied if
x>1+\delta_{0}
because then
z\notin\mathcal{R}
. Moreover, the condition on
y
is empty if
\kappa<(C_{1}C_{0}^{2}q)^{2/3}N^{-2/3}
.
Therefore, the proof proceeds by cutting the interval
[1,\infty)
as follows

[1,\infty)=[1,1+KN^{-2/3}]\cup(1+KN^{-2/3},1+\delta_{0}]\cup(1+\delta_{0},\infty),

(2.38)

for some
K\geq(C_{1}C_{0}^{2}q)^{2/3}
chosen explicitly below.
The main task is to control the number of particles in the second interval, which is done by cutting it into many pieces and applying (
2.37
) to each of them.
The first and third interval have to be treated differently.
In order to make the argument on the second interval work, we need to take
K
larger than the constraint mentioned above, more precisely, we choose

K\coloneqq(qL^{3})^{4a/3}\qquad\text{with}\qquad L\coloneqq C_{0}\vee C_{1}\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{2}C_{2}}}\right)^{1/4}\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(eC_{2}}}\right)^{1/2}\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(eC_{2}B_{\max}}}\right)^{1/(1+3a)}.
