\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert A_{N}(\chi\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{Cq^{2}N(\log N)^{2}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}}}\right)^{q}.

(5.21)

Combining (
5.13
), (
5.14
), (
5.15
) and (
5.21
) concludes the proof.
∎

5.3
Proof of the optimal local law

We prove here the following optimal local law under
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
uniformly in
\alpha
. This implies Theorem
1.5
by taking
\alpha=1
when
p>2
and
\alpha=0
when
p=2
.

Theorem 5.7

(Optimal local law)
.

Let
p>2
.
Let
\delta_{0}
,
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{T}
be as in Lemma
2.6
.
Then there exists
C>0
such that the following holds, for any
N,q\geq 1
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
.

(i)

For any
z\in\mathcal{T}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq)^{q/2}}{(Ny)^{q}}+\frac{(Cq)^{32q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z^{2}-1}}\right\rvert^{1/2})^{q}}.

(ii)

For any
z=\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{(Cq)^{64q}}{(Ny)^{q}}.

Proof.

The proof consists in applying four times the local law machinery, Proposition
2.7
. Between each application, we improve the bound we have on
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]
. There are two ways to improve this bound: we improve the dependence either on
z
or on
N
. Improving the dependence on
z
is done by applying the current best local law together with Helffer–Sjöstrand formula by applying Lemma
3.6
. Improving the dependence on
N
, is done via the master operator strategy, similarly to the proof of Lemma
5.6
.
Let
\delta_{0}
,
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{T}
be as in Lemma
2.6
.

First local law.
Applying Proposition
2.7
with the bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]
from Lemma
5.6
as input, we get: there exists
C>0
such that, for any
N\geq 2
,
q\geq 1
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\begin{cases}\dfrac{(Cq)^{q/2}}{(Ny)^{q}}+\dfrac{(Cq^{2}(\log N)^{2})^{q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z^{2}-1}}\right\rvert^{1/2})^{q}},&\text{if }z\in\mathcal{T},\\
\dfrac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{4}(\log N)^{4}}}\right)^{q}}{(Ny)^{q}},&\text{if }z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T},\end{cases}

which can be unified into the less precise bound, holding for any
z\in\mathcal{R}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{4}(\log N)^{4}}}\right)^{q}}{N^{q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{y}+\frac{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert^{2}}}}\right)^{q},

(5.22)

where we used that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert^{-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z^{2}-1}}\right\rvert^{-1/2}\leq C(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert^{-2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z-1}}\right\rvert^{-1/2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z+1}}\right\rvert^{-1/2})\leq C(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert^{-2}+y^{-1})
for any
z\in\mathcal{R}
.

Improvement of the bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]
via Helffer–Sjöstrand.

Applying first the truncation argument in (
5.7
) and then Lemma
3.6
with (
5.22
) as input, we deduce the following bound: for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}
, there exists
C>0
such that, for any
N\geq 2
,
q\geq 1
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
z\in K
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{4}(\log N)^{4}}}\right)^{q}.

(5.23)

Second local law.
Applying Proposition
2.7
with the bound (
5.23
) as input, we get: there exists
C>0
such that, for any
N\geq 2
,
q\geq 1
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\begin{cases}\dfrac{(Cq)^{q/2}}{(Ny)^{q}}+\dfrac{(Cq^{4}(\log N)^{4})^{q}}{(N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z^{2}-1}}\right\rvert^{1/2})^{q}},&\text{if }z\in\mathcal{T},\\
\dfrac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{8}(\log N)^{8}}}\right)^{q}}{(Ny)^{q}},&\text{if }z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{T},\end{cases}

which can be unified into the less precise bound, holding for any
z\in\mathcal{R}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{8}(\log N)^{8}}}\right)^{q}}{(Ny)^{q}}.

(5.24)

Improvement of the bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]
via master operator.
We follow the argument used in the proof of Lemma
5.6
.
By (
5.13
) and (
5.14
),

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq(Cq)^{2q}+\frac{C^{q}}{N^{q}}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert A_{N}(\phi\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right],

(5.25)

where we choose here
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
that satisfies

\mathbbm{1}_{[-1-\delta_{0}/2,1+\delta_{0}/2]}\leq\phi\leq\mathbbm{1}_{[-1-3\delta_{0}/4,1+3\delta_{0}/4]},

and set again
\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z}\coloneqq\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f_{\alpha,z}\phi]
. For
\varepsilon\in(0,\delta_{0}/2)
, let
\Psi^{\varepsilon}_{\alpha,z}
be the regularization of
\phi\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z}
given by Lemma
4.5
.
Proceeding as for the proof of (
5.21
), but using the local law (
5.24
) as input to apply Lemma
3.4
with
K_{0}=[-1-\delta_{0},1+\delta_{0}]
and
M=Cq^{8}(\log N)^{8}
(note that
\supp\Psi^{\varepsilon}_{\alpha,z}\subset K_{0}
by our choice of
\phi
and
\varepsilon
), we get the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert A_{N}(\phi\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert A_{N}(\phi\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z}-\Psi^{\varepsilon}_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert A_{N}(\Psi^{\varepsilon}_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{CN^{2}\varepsilon^{1-\gamma}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}}}\right)^{q}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{16}(\log N)^{16}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\gamma}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}+\frac{\log(1/\eta)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}+\frac{\eta}{\varepsilon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}}}\right)}}\right)^{q}.

We now take
\varepsilon=\eta=N^{-2/(1-\gamma)}
, so that
N^{2}\varepsilon^{1-\gamma}=1
, and this yields

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert A_{N}(\phi\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{Cq^{16}(\log N)^{17}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}}}\right)^{q}.

(5.26)

Coming back to (
5.25
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{Cq^{16}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert}}}\right)^{q}.

(5.27)

Third local law.
Applying Proposition
2.7
with the bound (
5.27
) as input and unifying the cases as for (
5.22
), we get: there exists
C>0
such that, for any
N\geq 2
,
q\geq 1
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
z\in\mathcal{R}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert s_{N}(z)-s_{V_{\alpha}}(z)}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{32}}}\right)^{q}}{N^{q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{y}+\frac{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert z}}\right\rvert^{2}}}}\right)^{q}.

(5.28)

Improvement of the bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]
via Helffer–Sjöstrand.

Applying first the truncation argument in (
5.7
) and then Lemma
3.6
with (
5.28
) as input, we deduce the following bound: for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}
, there exists
C>0
such that, for any
N\geq 2
,
q\geq 1
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
z\in K
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert L_{N}(f_{\alpha,z})}}\right\rvert^{q}}}\right]\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Cq^{32}}}\right)^{q}.

(5.29)

Fourth local law.
Applying Proposition
2.7
with the bound (
5.29
) as input, we get the desired result.
∎

6
Central limit theorem

We are now able to give a proof of Theorem
1.1
, but before that we state a preliminary lemma to rewrite the limiting mean and variance appearing in the proof.

Lemma 6.1

(Mean/variance formulas)
.

Let
p>2
.
Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\psi_{\alpha}=\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f]
. Then,

\int_{-1}^{1}\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\\
=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)}{r_{\alpha}(x)}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{f(t)-f(x)}{t-x}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}\sigma(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x-\frac{f(1)+f(-1)}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}{\sigma(x)}

(6.1)

and

\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}V^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x)^{2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)+\dfrac{1}{2}\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{\psi_{\alpha}(x)-\psi_{\alpha}(y)}{x-y}}}\right)^{2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(y)\\
=\dfrac{1}{\pi^{2}}\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}}}\right)^{2}\dfrac{1-xy}{\sigma(x)\sigma(y)}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y.

(6.2)

Proof.

First recall that, by Lemma
4.1
, for any
x\in[-1,1]
,

\psi_{\alpha}(x)=-\frac{1}{\pi r_{\alpha}(x)}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{f(t)-f(x)}{t-x}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)},

and
\psi_{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
so that all terms in the claimed equalities make sense. Moreover, it follows from Lemma
4.1
.
(ii)
that
\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}
is Hölder continuous, which is used in the proof of (
6.2
).

Proof of (
6.1
).

Recalling from (
1.24
) that
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\sigma(x)r_{\alpha}(x)\mathbbm{1}_{[-1,1]}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x
and differentiating the expression of
\psi_{\alpha}
given above, we find

\int_{-1}^{1}\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)}{r_{\alpha}(x)}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{f(t)-f(x)}{t-x}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}\sigma(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\\
-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{f(t)-f(x)}{t-x}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}}}\right)\sigma(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x.

Integrating by parts and using
\sigma^{\prime}(x)=-x/\sigma(x)
, the second term rewrites

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{f(t)-f(x)}{t-x}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}}}\right)\sigma(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\frac{f(t)-f(x)}{t-x}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}\frac{x}{\sigma(x)}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\frac{f(1)+f(-1)}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}{\sigma(x)},

by
[
43
, Lemma A.5]
, concluding the proof of (
6.1
).

Proof of (
6.2
).

By
[
9
, Lemma 4.2]
(note that they define
\psi_{\alpha}
with an additional factor
\frac{1}{2}
compared to us), we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}V^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x)^{2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)+\dfrac{1}{2}\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{\psi_{\alpha}(x)-\psi_{\alpha}(y)}{x-y}}}\right)^{2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(y)

\displaystyle=-\int_{-1}^{1}\psi_{\alpha}(x)f^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x).

(6.3)

This fact uses that
\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}
is Hölder continuous (this ensures that the integrals in the intermediate steps of the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 4.2]
are finite).
On the other hand, it can be noticed that the integral on the RHS of (
6.3
) can be recast as a weighted
\mathsf{H}^{1/2}
-norm as follows. Using the formula for
\psi_{\alpha}(x)
recalled above, that
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\sigma(x)r_{\alpha}(x)\mathbbm{1}_{[-1,1]}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x
, and Fubini’s theorem, we get

\int_{-1}^{1}\psi_{\alpha}(x)f^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)=\dfrac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y}{\sigma(y)}\int_{-1}^{1}\dfrac{\sigma(x)f^{\prime}(x)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f(x)-f(y)}}\right)}{y-x}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x.

Now using that:

\partial_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(f(x)-f(y))^{2}}}\right]=2f^{\prime}(x)(f(x)-f(y))\hskip 28.45274pt{\mathrm{and}}\hskip 28.45274pt\partial_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\dfrac{\sigma(x)}{y-x}}}\right]=\dfrac{1-xy}{\sigma(x)(x-y)^{2}},

we find by integration by parts:

\dfrac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y}{\sigma(y)}\int_{-1}^{1}\dfrac{\sigma(x)f^{\prime}(x)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f(x)-f(y)}}\right)}{y-x}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=-\dfrac{1}{\pi^{2}}\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}}}\right)^{2}\dfrac{1-xy}{\sigma(x)\sigma(y)}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y,

from which we conclude.
∎

We now state and prove a CLT under
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
, which implies Theorem
1.1
by taking
\alpha=1
for
p>2
and
\alpha=0
for
p=2
.

Theorem 6.2

(CLT)
.

Let
p>2
. For any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
with at most exponential growth we have for all
k\geq 1
, uniformly in
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[L_{N}(f)^{k}}}\right]\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}M_{k}^{\alpha}(f),

where
M_{k}^{\alpha}(f)
is the
k
-th moment of a normal distribution of mean and variance given by

\displaystyle m_{V_{\alpha}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{1}{\beta}}}\right)\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f]^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)

(6.4)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(f)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\beta\pi^{2}}\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}}}\right)^{2}\cdot\dfrac{1-xy}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\sqrt{1-y^{2}}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y.

(6.5)

Proof.

Let
f
be
\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
.
By Lemma
5.3
.
(ii)
, we know that the moments of
L_{N}(f)
for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
with at most exponential growth are approximated by those of
L_{N}(f\phi)
where
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, so it is now enough to work with
f
with compact support.
This ensures that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}}<\infty
.
We set
\psi_{\alpha}\coloneqq\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f]
.
Recall from Lemma
4.1
that
\psi_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
and satisfies
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq C
and, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C(1+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{-\gamma})
for some
\gamma\in[0,1)
(one can choose
\gamma=(3-p)_{+}
if
p\neq 3
and some arbitrary
\gamma\in(0,1)
if
p=3
).
Note that nothing else on
\psi_{\alpha}
and
f
are used afterwards

The proof is based on the change of variables method in the partition function, as in the proof of Lemma
5.6
, but in a more precise version.
Making the change of variables
\lambda_{i}=y_{i}+\psi_{\alpha}(y_{i})/N
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
in the partition function, as in (
5.10
) and then rewriting the integral as an expectation, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl(\dfrac{\beta}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 1+\dfrac{1}{N}\dfrac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})-\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{j})}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}}}\right\rvert\\
-\dfrac{\beta N}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[V_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda_{i}+\dfrac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})}{N}}}\right)-V_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})}}\right]+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\dfrac{\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})}{N}}}\right)\Biggr)\Biggr]=1.

We then proceed to a Taylor expansion as in Lemma
5.6
but one order further, using in particular that, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, by decomposing
V_{\alpha}=\alpha V+(1-\alpha)V_{0}
, using (
A.16
) for the part involving
V
and noting that the part involving
V_{0}
is zero,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert V_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda+\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)}{N}}}\right)-V_{\alpha}(\lambda)-\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)}{N}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda)-\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{2}}{2N^{2}}V_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert^{3}}{N^{3}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda+\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)}{N}}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert}}\right)^{p-3}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert^{p}}{N^{p}}+\mathbbm{1}_{p>3}C\alpha\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert^{3}}{N^{3}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-3},

using that, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert y}}\right\rvert\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x-y}}\right\rvert/2
when
p\leq 3
, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert y}}\right\rvert\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x-y}}\right\rvert+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert y}}\right\rvert
if
p>3
.
This yields that

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})-\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{j})}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})+I_{N}+\mathrm{Rem}^{(2)}_{N}}}\right)}}\right]=1,

where

\displaystyle I_{N}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{\beta}{4N^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})-\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{j})}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}}}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})-\frac{\beta}{4N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})^{2}V_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathrm{Rem}^{(2)}_{N}}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{3}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}}\right)+\frac{C}{N^{p-2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p}+\mathbbm{1}_{p>3}\frac{C}{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-3}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0,

using that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}<\infty
and, for the last term, that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C/(\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-1})
for
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert\geq 2
by definition of
\psi_{\alpha}
in (
4.1
) together with the bound (
4.4
) on the denominator.
Next, we use (
1.23
), with
h=\psi_{\alpha}
to deduce that

\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})-\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{j})}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})=\beta L_{N}(\Xi_{\alpha}[\psi_{\alpha}])-\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})+\frac{\beta}{2N}A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha}),

to get that

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta L_{N}(f)+I_{N}^{\prime}+\frac{\beta}{2N}A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})}}\right)}}\right]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1,

where we set

\displaystyle I_{N}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneqq I_{N}-\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{4N^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})-\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{j})}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}}}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\beta}{2}}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})-\frac{\beta}{4N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})^{2}V_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{i}).

We now use the large deviations for the empirical measure
\mu_{N}
to justify that we can replace the term
I_{N}^{\prime}
by its limit.
This argument is classical: by
[
3
, Theorem 2.6.1]
, and for a metric
d
compatible with the weak convergence of probability measures, for any
\delta>0
, there exists
c>0
such that, for
N
large enough and for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}(d(\mu_{N},\mu_{V_{\alpha}})\geq\delta)\leq e^{-cN^{2}}.

The uniformity in
\alpha
is not claimed in
[
3
, Theorem 2.6.1]
but can be obtained by adapting their argument.
Since all the terms involved in
I_{N}^{\prime}
are continuous functions with respect to
d
of the empirical measure
\mu_{N}
(as integrals of bounded continuous functions tested against
\mu_{N}
), and by the crude bound

\beta L_{N}(f)+\frac{\beta}{2N}A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})=O(N),

decomposing on the events
\{d(\mu_{N},\mu_{V_{\alpha}})<\delta\}
and
\{d(\mu_{N},\mu_{V_{\alpha}})\geq\delta\}
, then letting
N\rightarrow\infty
and then
\delta\rightarrow 0
, we conclude that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta L_{N}(f)+\frac{\beta}{2N}A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})}}\right)}}\right]=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta m_{V_{\alpha}}(f)+\dfrac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sigma^{2}(f)}}\right),

where we used Lemma
6.1
to rewrite the limit of
I_{N}^{\prime}
in terms of
m_{V_{\alpha}}(f)
and
\sigma^{2}(f)
.
Since this convergence holds for
tf
instead of
f
for any fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
, this proves the convergence of the Laplace transform of
L_{N}(f)+\frac{1}{2N}A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})
to the Laplace transform of
\mathcal{N}(m_{V_{\alpha}}(f),\sigma^{2}(f))
.
Since the convergence of Laplace transform implies the convergence of moments, it only remains to show that the moments of
\frac{1}{N}A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})
converge to zero,
i.e.
that for all
q\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{1}{N}A_{N}(\psi_{\alpha})}}\right|^{q}}}\right]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0.

(6.6)

This was already proved for
\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z}
instead of
\psi_{\alpha}
, by first truncating outside of
[-1-\delta_{0},1+\delta_{0}]
in (
5.14
), and then proving the result for the truncated version of
\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha,z}
in (
5.26
).
Recalling that
\psi_{\alpha}
and
\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}
are bounded and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C(1+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{-\gamma})
with
\gamma\in[0,1)
, the same argument works to prove (
6.6
).
This concludes the proof.
∎

We proved the central limit theorem in moments for a general function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
with at most exponential growth but we can also prove it for the test function
\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}
. This will allow us in the next section to deduce the subleading correction of the free energy. As we only need the convergence of the first moment, we don’t state the full central limit theorem.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
p>2
.
The following convergence holds uniformly in
\alpha\in[0,1]
:

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[L_{N}(\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha})}}\right]\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}m_{V_{\alpha}}(\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha})

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
6.2
, we first truncate the function
\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}
to replace it by the function
f=\phi\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
with compact support.
Let
\psi_{\alpha}=\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f]
. As mentioned at the beginning of the proof of Theorem
6.2
, the CLT in terms of moment holds as soon as we have
\psi_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq C
and, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{-\gamma}+1)
for some
\gamma\in(0,1]
.
This can be showed by following the proof of Lemma
4.3
, noting that
f
satisfies better bounds than
f_{\alpha,z}
, that is
f,f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}
are bounded and
|f^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)|\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{-(3-p)_{+}}
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

7
Subleading order of the partition function

As we have obtained the subleading order for a general linear statistic
L_{N}(f)
, we now compute the next order in the asymptotic expansion of the partition function with Freud weights
Z_{N}
, see Theorem
1.3
. We call
Z_{N}^{G}
the following partition function:

Z_{N}^{G}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\prod_{i<j}|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|^{\beta}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-N\frac{\beta}{2}V_{G}(\lambda_{i})}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}

(7.1)

with
V_{G}(x)\coloneqq 2x^{2}
. Under the associated probability distribution
\mathbb{P}_{N}^{G}
, the empirical distribution
\mu_{N}=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\lambda_{i}}
converges towards the following semi-circle distribution

\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{G}(x)=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert\leq 1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x.

Thanks to Mehta’s formula
[
47
, 17.6.7]
, the partition function
Z_{N}^{G}
is known explicitly:

Z_{N}^{G}=\dfrac{N!(2\pi)^{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{2}}}\right)^{(\frac{\beta}{2}-1)N}}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{2}}}\right)^{N}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N+1,\frac{2}{\beta},1}}\right)}\cdot(4N)^{-\frac{\beta}{4}N^{2}-(1-\frac{\beta}{2})\frac{N}{2}},

(7.2)

where
\Gamma(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
stands for the Barnes double Gamma function, see
[
17
, Section 7.2.2]
.
From the previous formula, we can explicitly deduce the all-order asymptotic expansion of
Z_{N}^{G}
that we only write till
o(N)
.

Lemma 7.1

(Asymptotic expansion of
\log Z_{N}^{G}
)
.

As
N\to\infty
,

\log Z_{N}^{G}=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\dfrac{\beta}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{3}{4}+\log 2}}\right)}}\right]N^{2}+\dfrac{\beta}{2}N\log N+F^{\{-1\}}_{G}\beta N+o(N),

(7.3)

where

F^{\{-1\}}_{G}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{1}{\beta}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\dfrac{\beta}{2}+\log 2}}\right)-\dfrac{1}{2\beta}-\dfrac{1}{4}+\dfrac{1}{\beta}\log\dfrac{2\pi}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta/2}}\right)}.

(7.4)

For the next lemma, we recall that
V_{\alpha}=\alpha V+(1-\alpha)V_{G}
.

Lemma 7.2

(Interpolation formula)
.

For any
N\geq 1
, we have

\log Z_{N}=\log Z_{N}^{G}-\dfrac{N^{2}\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{N}(x)}}\right]\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\alpha.

Proof.

Denoting
Z_{N}^{\alpha}
the partition function associated with the potential
V_{\alpha}
, we have

\log\dfrac{Z_{N}}{Z_{N}^{G}}=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}\log Z_{N}^{\alpha}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\alpha=-\dfrac{N^{2}\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{N}(x)}}\right]\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\alpha,

by inserting
\partial_{\alpha}
inside the integral in the definition of
Z_{N}^{\alpha}
.
∎

To recast a term in the asymptotic expansion of
\log Z_{N}
as the entropy of
\mu_{V}
,
i.e.

\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
for
\alpha=1
, we need the following symmetry property verified by
\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

For any
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\int_{-1}^{1}f^{\prime}(x)\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[g](x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}g^{\prime}(x)\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f](x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x).

Proof.

From (
1.22
), we see that using the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}
:

\Xi_{\alpha}[f](x)=\mathcal{H}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f\rho_{V_{\alpha}}}}\right](x)=\fint_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{f(t)}{t-x}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(t).

\mathcal{H}
seen as bounded operator on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
,
1<p<\infty
, satisfies for
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
and
v\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R})
with
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
,

\braket{\mathcal{H}[u],v}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=-\braket{u,\mathcal{H}[v]}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

It also verifies
\mathcal{H}[u^{\prime}]=\mathcal{H}[u]^{\prime}
for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
such that
u^{\prime}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
,
p>1
; see for example
[
38
]
. Using these facts and by integration by parts, we get

\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}[f]^{\prime}(x)g(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x)=\braket{\mathcal{H}[(f\rho_{V_{\alpha}})^{\prime}],g\rho_{V_{\alpha}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=-\braket{(f\rho_{V_{\alpha}})^{\prime},\mathcal{H}[g\rho_{V_{\alpha}}]}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\\
=\braket{f\rho_{V_{\alpha}},\mathcal{H}[g\rho_{V_{\alpha}}]^{\prime}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}[g]^{\prime}(x)f(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x).

We used that,
(f\rho_{V_{\alpha}})^{\prime}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
for
1<p<2
and that
g\rho_{V_{\alpha}}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R})
for
q>2
. Now, since
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and because of Lemma
4.1
taking
\widetilde{f}=\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f]
and
\widetilde{g}=\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[g]
yields the result.
∎

The following theorem is a direct consequence of the asymptotic expansion of
\log Z_{N}^{G}
and Theorem
6.2
.

Theorem 7.4

(Subleading correction for the free energy)
.

As
N\rightarrow\infty
,

\dfrac{1}{N^{2}\beta}\log Z_{N}=-\dfrac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log 2+\dfrac{3}{2p}}}\right)+\dfrac{\log N}{2N}+\dfrac{F^{\{-1\}}}{N}+o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{1}{N}}}\right)

with

F^{\{-1\}}=F_{G}^{\{-1\}}-\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}m_{V_{\alpha}}(\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha})\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\alpha=F_{G}^{\{-1\}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{1}{\beta}-\dfrac{1}{2}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathrm{Ent}}[\mu_{V}]-\log\pi+\dfrac{1}{2}}}\right)

where
m_{V_{\alpha}}
was introduced in (
6.4
) and for all
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}),

{\mathrm{Ent}}[\mu]\coloneqq-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)}{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Proof.

The fact that the first order of the free energy is given by

\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}\dfrac{1}{N^{2}\beta}\log Z_{N}=-\frac{1}{2}\inf\limits_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\mathcal{E}(\mu)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(\mu_{V})

can be proven by standard large deviation techniques, see
[
3
, Theorem 2.6.1]
. From
[
24
, Lemma 4.1]
, we have that
-\mathcal{E}(\mu_{V})=\log 2+\frac{3}{2p}
.
Finally by Lemmas
7.2
and
7.1
and Theorem
6.2
, we can identify the terms proportional to
N\log N
and
N
as well as the size of the remainder. We find

F^{\{-1\}}-F_{G}^{\{-1\}}=-\dfrac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\dfrac{1}{\beta}}}\right)\int_{0}^{1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\alpha\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[V_{\alpha}]^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(x).

Following
[
31
, Theorem 4.28]
,
[
30
, Lemma 6.3]
it remains to show that this double integral can be recasted in terms of
{\mathrm{Ent}}[\mu_{V}]
. By differentiating (
1.23
) with respect to
\alpha\in[0,1]
, we have:

-\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}[h](x)\partial_{\alpha}\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}V^{\prime}_{\alpha}(x)h(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=0

for any
h\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1])
.
Now, replacing
h
with
-\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[f]
with
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and using Lemma
7.3
, we find:

\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)\partial_{\alpha}\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x-\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}](x)f^{\prime}(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=0

(7.5)

where we used that
\Xi_{\alpha}[h]=f-a
for some constant
a
and that
\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=0
.
By integrating by parts, we find:

\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}](x)f^{\prime}(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=-\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}]^{\prime}(x)f(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\\
-\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}](x)f(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x.

(7.6)

We can extend the previous relation to
f=\log\rho_{V_{\alpha}}
by density, since it is smooth and has singularity at most like
O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log(1-x^{2})}}\right)
at the endpoints. Thus, we obtain by integrating by parts twice, then using (
7.6
) and (
7.5
) and the fact that
\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=0
:

\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}]^{\prime}(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}](x)\dfrac{\rho_{V_{\alpha}}^{\prime}(x)}{\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)}\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\\
=\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}]^{\prime}(x)\log\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x+\int_{-1}^{1}\Xi_{\alpha}^{-1}[\partial_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}](x)\log\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x\\
=-2\int_{-1}^{1}\log\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\partial_{\alpha}\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=-2\partial_{\alpha}\int_{-1}^{1}\log\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\rho_{V_{\alpha}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x=2\partial_{\alpha}{\mathrm{Ent}}[\mu_{V_{\alpha}}].

Noticing that
{\mathrm{Ent}}[\mu_{G}]=\log\pi-\frac{1}{2}
is enough to conclude.
∎

8
Schatten balls: KLS conjecture, volume of unit balls

8.1
Consistency check of the KLS conjecture

The goal of this section is to show Theorem
1.10
. We consider
p\geq 2
and we denote by
X
a uniformly distributed random variable on
B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{N}}
.
To have more compact expressions, we define

G_{r,q}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{q}}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\lambda_{k}^{r}}}\right)^{q}}}\right].

We also recall that
d_{N}=\frac{\beta}{2}N(N-1)+N
where
\beta=1,2,4
when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
and that
c_{p}
was introduced in (
1.5
).

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
r
be an even integer,
q\in[0,\infty)
and
f(X)={\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q}
for all
X\in B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})
.
Then

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f(X)}}\right]=\frac{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{d_{N}}{p}}}\right)}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{d_{N}+rq}{p}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta Nc_{p}}{2}}}\right)^{rq/p}N^{q}G_{r,q}.

Proof.

Note that
f(X)=\sigma_{r}(X)^{q}
depends only on the eigenvalues of
X
.
Seen as a function of the eigenvalues, it is symmetric and positively homogeneous of degree
rq
. Hence Weyl’s formula Lemma
1.8
applies and, together with (
1.16
), it gives the result.
∎

As our goal is to establish the KLS conjecture in a specific case, we have to consider the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of the gradient of
f
.

Lemma 8.2

(Bound on the gradient)
.

Let
r
be an even integer,
q\in[1,\infty)
and
f(X)={\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q}
for all
X\in B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})
. Then

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\nabla f(X)\|_{{\mathrm{HS}}}^{2}}}\right]\geq(rq)^{2}\dfrac{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u}}\right)}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u+v_{2}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{\beta Nc_{p}}{2}}}\right)^{v_{2}}N^{2q-1}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{2q-2}\braket{\mu_{N},x^{2r-2}}}}\right],

with
u=1+d_{N}/p
and
v_{2}=2(rq-1)/p
.

Proof.

We view
X
as an element of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{N}}
, as
\mathbb{F}
is a
\beta
-dimensional real vector space. We denote by
\partial_{i,j,k}
the partial derivative with respect to the
k
-th real component of the entry
X_{ij}
. For
i>j
and
k\in\llbracket 1,\beta\rrbracket
, we have

\partial_{i,j,k}f(X)=q{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q-1}\cdot r{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r-1}\partial_{i,j,k}X}}\right)=rq{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q-1}\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(X^{r-1})_{ab}(\partial_{i,j,k}X)_{ba}.

But
\partial_{i,j,k}X=e_{k}E_{ij}+(\mathbbm{1}_{k=1}-\mathbbm{1}_{k>1})e_{k}E_{ji}
where
e_{k}
is the
k
-th unit vector of
\mathbb{F}
seen as a real vector space and
E_{ij}
is the
N\times N
matrix with 1 in the entry
(ij)
and 0 otherwise.
Hence, using also that
X
is self-adjoint, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(X^{r-1})_{ab}(\partial_{i,j,k}X)_{ba}

\displaystyle=e_{k}(X^{r-1})_{ij}^{*}+(\mathbbm{1}_{k=1}-\mathbbm{1}_{k>1})e_{k}(X^{r-1})_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{k=1}2e_{k}\re(X^{r-1})_{ij}+\mathbbm{1}_{k>1}2e_{k}\im_{\mathbb{F}}(X^{r-1})_{ij},

where, for
z\in\mathbb{F}
,
\re(z)
denotes its real part and
\im_{\mathbb{F}}(z)=z-\re(z)
(note that if
z\in\mathbb{C}
, then
\im_{\mathbb{F}}(z)=\mathrm{i}\im z
).
Therefore, we get, for
i>j
,

\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\partial_{i,j,k}f(X)}}\right\rvert^{2}\geq 4(rq{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q-1})^{2}\cdot\lvert(X^{r-1})_{ij}\rvert^{2}.

In the same way,

\partial_{i,i,1}f(X)=rq{\mathrm{Tr}}(X^{r})^{q-1}(X^{r-1})_{ii}
, thus

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\nabla f(X)}}\right\rVert_{{\mathrm{HS}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\partial_{i,i,1}f(X)}}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\partial_{i,j,k}f(X)}}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(rq{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q-1})^{2}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lvert(X^{r-1})_{ii}\rvert^{2}+4\sum_{i>j}\lvert(X^{r-1})_{ij}\rvert^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq(rq{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q-1})^{2}\cdot\sum_{i,j}\lvert(X^{r-1})_{ij}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=(rq{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q-1})^{2}\cdot{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{2r-2}}}\right).

Taking the expectation and using Weyl’s formula Lemma
1.8
with the homogeneous function
X\mapsto{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{2r-2}}}\right){\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{2q-2}
of degree
pv_{2}=2(rq-1)
yields the desired lower bound. ∎

The lower bound obtained in the previous lemma enables us to derive a manageable upper bound for the ratio we need to control to establish the KLS conjecture for our class of functions.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
r
be an even integer,
q\in[1,\infty)
and
f(X)={\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{r}}}\right)^{q}
, then

\dfrac{\Var{f(X)}}{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\nabla f(X)\|_{{\mathrm{HS}}}^{2}}}\right]\cdot\sup\limits_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d_{N}-1}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\braket{\theta,X}^{2}}}\right]}\leq\dfrac{d_{N}}{(rq)^{2}}\dfrac{\dfrac{G_{r,2q}}{\Gamma(u)\Gamma(u+2v_{0})}-\dfrac{G_{r,q}^{2}}{\Gamma(u+v_{0})^{2}}}{\dfrac{G_{2,1}}{\Gamma(u+v_{1})}\cdot\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{2q-2}\braket{\mu_{N},x^{2r-2}}]}{\Gamma(u+v_{2})}},

(8.1)

with
u=1+d_{N}/p
,
v_{0}=rq/p
,
v_{1}=2/p
and
v_{2}=2(rq-1)/p
.

Proof.

We first notice that

\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\braket{\theta,X}^{2}}}\right]=\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma)\geq\dfrac{{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)}{d_{N}}=\dfrac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|X\|_{{\mathrm{HS}}}^{2}}}\right]}{d_{N}},

where
\Sigma
is the covariance matrix of
X
.
Thus, by Lemma
8.1
with
r=2
and
q=1
,

\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma)\geq\dfrac{\Gamma(u)}{d_{N}\Gamma(u+v_{1})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{\beta Nc_{p}}{2}}}\right)^{v_{1}}NG_{2,1}.

Furthermore, applying Lemma
8.1
twice, we get

\Var{f(X)}=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f(X)^{2}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f(X)}}\right]^{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{\beta Nc_{p}}{2}}}\right)^{2v_{0}}N^{2q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{\Gamma(u)}{\Gamma(u+2v_{0})}G_{r,2q}-\dfrac{\Gamma(u)^{2}}{\Gamma(u+v_{0})^{2}}G_{r,q}^{2}}}\right).

Finally, Lemma
8.2
provides a lower bound on
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\nabla f(X)\|_{{\mathrm{HS}}}^{2}}}\right]
and, combining the different blocks and using
v_{1}+v_{2}=2v_{0}
, yields the result.
∎

In the following lemma, we compute explicitly the moments of the equilibrium measure
\mu_{V}
. This will be needed to compute the limit of the quantities
G_{r,q}
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
k
be an integer, then

\braket{\mu_{V},x^{2k}}=2^{-2k}\binom{2k}{k}\cdot\dfrac{p}{p+2k}.

Proof.

As in
[
57
, Lemma 4.1]
,
\braket{\mu_{V},x^{2k}}=\mathbb{E}[A^{2k}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[B^{2k}]
where
A
and
B
are independent with respectively arcsine distribution on
(-1,1)
and Beta
(p,1)
. Thus, since

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[A^{2k}}}\right]=\dfrac{(2k)!}{2^{2k}(k!)^{2}}=4^{-k}\binom{2k}{k},\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[B^{2k}}}\right]=\dfrac{p}{p+2k},

we conclude.
∎

The asymptotic variance of the linear statistics will also be needed to analyze the ratio appearing in Lemma
8.3
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
r
be an even integer and
q\in[1,\infty)
, then

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}d_{N}\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{q}}}\right)\leq\dfrac{rq^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{4^{r-1}}\binom{2r-2}{r-1}.

Proof.

First note that, if
q>1
, then by (
A.17
), there exists
C>0
depending on
q
such that for any
a>0
and
h\geq-a
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert(a+h)^{q}-a^{q}-qa^{q-1}h}}\right\rvert\leq Ch^{2}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a+h}}\right\rvert)^{q-2}\leq Ch^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a}}\right\rvert^{q-2}+Ch^{q}\mathbbm{1}_{q>2},

bounding
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a+h}}\right\rvert\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a}}\right\rvert
when
q\leq 2
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a+h}}\right\rvert\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a}}\right\rvert+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert h}}\right\rvert
when
q>2
.
Note that the bound above is also true for
q=1
because the LHS is simply zero.
Therefore, if we decompose

\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{q}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}+\dfrac{L_{N}(x^{r})}{N}}}\right)^{q}\eqqcolon\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{q}+q\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{q-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{L_{N}(x^{r})}{N}+\mathrm{Rem}^{(3)}_{N}}}\right),

then the remainder satisfies, with a constant
C
depending on
q
and
a=\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}\in(0,\infty)
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathrm{Rem}^{(3)}_{N}}}\right\rvert\leq C\dfrac{L_{N}(x^{r})^{2}}{N^{2}}+C\dfrac{L_{N}(x^{r})^{q}}{N^{q}}\mathbbm{1}_{q>2}.

We obtain

\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{q}}}\right)=q^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{L_{N}(x^{r})}{N}+\mathrm{Rem}^{(3)}_{N}}}\right)\underset{N\to\infty}{\sim}\dfrac{\beta q^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{2d_{N}}\sigma^{2}(x^{r}),

using
N^{2}\sim 2d_{N}/\beta
and that
L_{N}(x^{r})
converges in terms of moments towards
\mathcal{N}(m_{V}(x^{r}),\sigma^{2}(x^{r}))
by Theorem
1.1
.
Now if
A
and
B
are two independent arcsine (thus symmetric) distributions on
(-1,1)
, we then have from the definition of
\sigma^{2}
:

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}d_{N}\Var{\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{q}}=\dfrac{\beta q^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{2}\sigma^{2}(x^{r})=\dfrac{q^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}A^{r-1-i}B^{i}}}\right)^{2}(1-AB)}}\right],

Furthermore, we have by the crude bound
1-AB\leq 2
, symmetry and independence:

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}A^{r-1-i}B^{i}}}\right)^{2}(1-AB)}}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\#\{(k,\ell)\in\llbracket 0,r-1\rrbracket^{2}:k+\ell=2i\}A^{2r-2-2i}B^{2i}}}\right]\\
\leq 2r\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}A^{2r-2-2i}B^{2i}}}\right].

Recalling that
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[A^{2k}}}\right]=4^{-k}\binom{2k}{k}
, we obtain:

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}d_{N}\Var(\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{q})\leq 2r\dfrac{q^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[A^{2r-2-2i}}}\right]\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[B^{2i}}}\right]\\
=2r\dfrac{q^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\dfrac{\binom{2r-2-2i}{r-1-i}}{4^{r-1-i}}\cdot\dfrac{\binom{2i}{i}}{4^{i}}=\dfrac{rq^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{4^{r-1}}\binom{2r-2}{r-1}

by using the Vandermonde identity, which is the desired bound.
∎

We are now able to give the consistency check on the KLS conjecture.

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

We start from Lemma
8.3
and bound first the numerator
\mathcal{N}_{N}
of the RHS of (
8.1
). We begin

\dfrac{\mathcal{N}_{N}}{d_{N}}\coloneqq\dfrac{G_{r,2q}}{\Gamma(u)\Gamma(u+2v_{0})}-\dfrac{G_{r,q}^{2}}{\Gamma(u+v_{0})^{2}}\\
=\dfrac{\Var{\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{q}}}{\Gamma(u)\Gamma(u+2v_{0})}+\dfrac{G_{r,q}^{2}}{\Gamma(u)^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{\Gamma(u)}{\Gamma(u+2v_{0})}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dfrac{\Gamma(u)}{\Gamma(u+v_{0})}}}\right)^{2}}}\right)\eqqcolon(1)+(2),

recalling
u=1+d_{N}/p
and
v_{0}=rq/p
.
Using Lemma
8.5
,
\Gamma(u+2v_{0})\sim u^{2v_{0}}\Gamma(u)
and Stirling’s formula
\Gamma(u)\sim u^{u}e^{-u}\sqrt{2\pi/u}
, we have as
N\rightarrow\infty
:

(1)\leq\dfrac{rq^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{4^{r-1}}\binom{2r-2}{r-1}\frac{u^{-2u-2v_{0}}e^{2u}}{2\pi p}(1+o(1)).

Using also

\dfrac{\Gamma(u+v_{0})}{\Gamma(u)}=u^{v_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\dfrac{v_{0}(v_{0}-1)}{2u}+O(u^{-2})}}\right),

one obtains:

(2)\sim-\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q}\dfrac{v_{0}^{2}}{2\pi}u^{-2u-2v_{0}}e^{2u}<0.

Thus, as
N\rightarrow\infty
,

\dfrac{\mathcal{N}_{N}}{d_{N}}\leq\dfrac{u^{-2u-2v_{0}}e^{2u}}{4^{r-1}}\cdot\dfrac{rq^{2}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}}{2\pi p}\binom{2r-2}{r-1}(1+o(1)).

(8.2)

Finally, we need to study the denominator
\mathcal{D}_{N}
of the RHS of (
8.1
):

\dfrac{\mathcal{D}_{N}}{(rq)^{2}}\coloneqq\dfrac{G_{2,1}\mathbb{E}[\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{2q-2}\braket{\mu_{N},x^{2r-2}}]}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u+v_{1}}}\right)\Gamma(u+v_{2})},

with
v_{1}=2/p
and
v_{2}=2(rq-1)/p
.
First, by the law of large numbers for
\mu_{N}
and then Lemma
8.4
,

G_{2,1}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\braket{\mu_{N},x^{r}}^{2q-2}\braket{\mu_{N},x^{2r-2}}}}\right]\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{2}}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{2r-2}}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}\\
=2\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}\cdot 4^{-r}\dfrac{p}{p+2}\dfrac{p}{p+2r-2}\binom{2r-2}{r-1}.

On the other hand, using previously mentioned properties of
\Gamma
and noticing that
v_{1}+v_{2}=2v_{0}
, we obtain:

\dfrac{1}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u+v_{1}}}\right)\Gamma(u+v_{2})}\sim\dfrac{u^{-2u-2v_{0}+1}e^{2u}}{2\pi}.

We thus deduce that as
N\rightarrow\infty
:

\dfrac{\mathcal{D}_{N}}{(rq)^{2}}\sim\dfrac{u^{-2u-2v_{0}+1}e^{2u}}{\pi}\braket{\mu_{V},x^{r}}^{2q-2}\cdot 4^{-r}\dfrac{p}{p+2}\dfrac{p}{p+2r-2}\binom{2r-2}{r-1}

(8.3)

and thus combining (
8.2
) and (
8.3
),

\dfrac{\mathcal{N}_{N}}{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\leq\dfrac{2}{r}\dfrac{p+2}{p}\dfrac{p+2r-2}{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+o(1)}}\right)\leq 4+o(1),

noting that the first bound obtained is decreasing in
p
, and so is maximized for
p=2
. This yields the desired claim.
∎

8.2
Volume of Schatten balls

In this subsection, we prove Corollary
1.9
which gives an asymptotic expansion for the volume of the unit Schatten balls
B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})
.

Proof of Corollary
1.9
.

By (
1.16
) and Theorem
1.3
, we have as
N\rightarrow\infty
,

\log\lvert B_{E}(S_{p}^{N})\rvert=\frac{d_{N}}{p}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta Nc_{p}}{2}}}\right)+\log c_{N}-\log\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{d_{N}}{p}}}\right)-N^{2}\frac{\beta}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log 2+\dfrac{3}{2p}}}\right)\\
+\frac{\beta}{2}N\log N+N\beta F^{\{-1\}}+o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N}}\right),

(8.4)

where we recall that
d_{N}=\beta N(N-1)/2+N
and we develop each term in terms of
N
. We find the asymptotic of
\log c_{N}
as in
[
24
, Lemma 4.7]
by using, by
[
3
, Proposition 4.1.14]
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathcal{U}_{N}(\mathbb{F})}}\right\rvert=\dfrac{(2\pi)^{\frac{\beta N(N+1)}{4}}2^{N(1-\frac{\beta}{2})}}{\prod_{k=1}^{N}\Gamma(\beta k/2)}.

Using Stirling’s formula

\log\Gamma(x)=x\log x-x-\dfrac{1}{2}\log x+\dfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)+o_{x\to+\infty}(1),

and the asymptotic expansions

\sum_{k=1}^{N}k\log k=\frac{1}{2}N^{2}\log N-\frac{1}{4}N^{2}+\frac{1}{2}N\log N+o(N),\hskip 28.45274pt\sum_{k=1}^{N}\log k=N\log N-N+o(N),

one gets from the definition of
c_{N}
, see Lemma
1.8
,

\log c_{N}=-\frac{\beta}{4}N^{2}\log N+\frac{\beta}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3}{2}+\log\frac{4\pi}{\beta}}}\right)N^{2}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{4}+\dfrac{1}{2}}}\right)N\log N+\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\Gamma(\beta/2)+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\log(\pi\beta)}}\right)-\dfrac{1}{2}\log\frac{4\pi}{\beta}}}\right)N+o(N).

By Stirling’s formula again,

\log\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{d_{N}}{p}}}\right)=\frac{\beta}{p}N^{2}\log N+\frac{\beta}{2p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{\beta}{2p}-1}}\right)N^{2}+\frac{2-\beta}{p}N\log N\\
+\frac{2-\beta}{2p}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{2p}}}\right)N+o(N).

The result then follows from collecting all the terms in (
8.4
).
∎

Appendix A
Proofs of technical estimates on functions

A.1
Preliminaries on an integral operator

In this subsection, we prove some basic facts concerning the regularity of an integral transformation of a function, which appears in various quantities, e.g. in the definition of
r_{\alpha}
(with
\mu
being the arcsine distribution) or in the definition of the master operator (with
\mu=\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
).

We introduce the following notation for Taylor expansions. If
\theta
is a function which is
n
times differentiable at
x
, we write

T_{n}[\theta,x](t)\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\theta^{(k)}(x)}{k!}(t-x)^{k},\qquad\text{for any }t\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.1)

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
[-1,1]
.
Let
\ell\geq 1
and
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
.
For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\Theta(x)\coloneqq\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\theta(t)-\theta(x)}{t-x}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t).

(A.2)

Then, the following properties hold.

(i)

The function
\Theta
is in
\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}(\mathbb{R})
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Theta}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\theta}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\ell}}
and, for any
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Theta^{(k)}(x)=k!\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\theta(t)-T_{k}[\theta,x](t)}{(t-x)^{k+1}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t).

(A.3)

Moreover,
\Theta
is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
and (
A.3
) holds with
k=\ell
and
x\notin[-1,1]
.

(ii)

If
\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{1}{1-t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)<\infty
, then
\Theta
is
\ell
times differentiable at 1 and (
A.3
) holds with
k=\ell
and
x=1
.

(iii)

If
\mu
has a density
\varrho
such that, for some
M>0
, for any
x\in[-1,1]
,
\varrho(x)\leq M\sqrt{1-x}
, and if, for any
x\geq 1
,
\theta(x)=T_{\ell}[\theta,1](x)
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\Theta^{(\ell)}(x)-\Theta^{(\ell)}(1)}}\right\rvert\begin{cases}\leq 8M(\ell+1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sup_{[-1,1]}\lvert\theta^{(\ell)}\rvert}}\right)(x-1)^{1/2},&\text{for }x\in[1,2],\\
=o((x-1)^{1/2}),&\text{as }x\downarrow 1.\end{cases}

(A.4)

Proof.

Part
(i)
. This is proved by induction using differentiation under the integral. For
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
the domination is given by

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\theta(t)-T_{k}[\theta,x](t)}{(t-x)^{k+1}}}}\right\rvert\leq\lVert\theta^{(k+1)}\rVert_{\infty},

which is an application of Taylor–Lagrange inequality.
For
k=\ell
and on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
, the domination is done using that
1/(t-x)
is bounded uniformly in
t\in[-1,1]
and
x
in a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
.

Part
(ii)
. First assume
\ell\geq 2
. Using Part
(i)
and
\frac{1}{t-x}=\frac{1}{t-1}+\frac{x-1}{(t-x)(t-1)}
, we have, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Theta^{(\ell-1)}(x)

\displaystyle=(\ell-1)!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\theta(t)-T_{\ell-1}[\theta,x](t)}{(t-x)^{\ell-1}}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}+(x-1)\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\theta(t)-T_{\ell-1}[\theta,x](t)}{(t-x)^{\ell}}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=(\ell-1)\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\theta^{(\ell-1)}(x+u(t-x))(1-u)^{\ell-2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}u\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}-\theta^{(\ell-1)}(x)\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+(x-1)(\ell-1)!\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\theta(t)-T_{\ell-1}[\theta,x](t)}{(t-x)^{\ell}}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1},

(A.5)

where, in the first integral, we decomposed
\theta(t)-T_{\ell-1}[\theta,x](t)=\theta(t)-T_{\ell-2}[\theta,x](t)-\frac{(t-x)^{\ell-1}}{(\ell-1)!}\theta^{(\ell-1)}(x)
and applied Taylor integral formula.
Note that the first term on the right-hand side is simply 0 when
\ell=1
.
Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\Theta^{(\ell-1)}(x)-\Theta^{(\ell-1)}(1)}{x-1}

\displaystyle=(\ell-1)\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\theta^{(\ell-1)}(x+u(t-x))-\theta^{(\ell-1)}(1+u(t-1))}{x-1}(1-u)^{\ell-2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}u\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\frac{\theta^{(\ell-1)}(x)-\theta^{(\ell-1)}(1)}{x-1}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}+(\ell-1)!\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\theta(t)-T_{\ell-1}[\theta,x](t)}{(t-x)^{\ell}}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[x\to 1]{}(\ell-1)\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\theta^{(\ell)}(1+u(t-1))(1-u)^{\ell-1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}u\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\theta^{(\ell)}(1)\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1}+(\ell-1)!\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\theta(t)-T_{\ell-1}[\theta,1](t)}{(t-1)^{\ell}}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{t-1},

(A.6)

by dominated convergence for the first and the third term, using Taylor–Lagrange inequality and the local boundedness of
\theta^{(\ell)}
for the domination. We also used here the assumption
\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{1}{1-t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)<\infty
.
Using again Taylor integral formula shows that the right-hand side of (
A.6
) equals the right-hand side of (
A.3
) with
k=\ell
and
x=1
.
The case
\ell=1
is similar, but one should use directly the first equality in (
A.5
), without rewriting further using Taylor integral formula.

Part
(iii)
. Using that
\theta(x)=T_{\ell}[\theta,1](x)
for any
x\geq 1
, we also have
\theta^{(k)}(x)=T_{\ell-k}[\theta^{(k)},1](x)
for any
0\leq k\leq\ell
and therefore
T_{\ell}[\theta,x](t)=T_{\ell}[\theta,1](t)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Using this in (
A.3
) and that, for
t<1<x
,
\lvert\frac{1}{(t-x)^{\ell+1}}-\frac{1}{(t-1)^{\ell+1}}\rvert\leq\frac{(\ell+1)(x-1)}{(x-t)(1-t)^{\ell+1}}
, we get

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\Theta^{(\ell)}(x)-\Theta^{(\ell)}(1)}}\right\rvert\leq\ell!\int_{-1}^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta(t)-T_{\ell}[\theta,1](t)}}\right\rvert\frac{(\ell+1)(x-1)}{(x-t)(1-t)^{\ell+1}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t).

(A.7)

Note that, for any
t\in[-1,1]
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta(t)-T_{\ell}[\theta,1](t)}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{2}{\ell!}(1-t)^{\ell}\sup_{[-1,1]}\lvert\theta^{(\ell)}\rvert
by Taylor–Lagrange inequality.
Moreover, using the assumption on
\mu
, we have, for any
x\in[1,2]
,

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{(x-t)(1-t)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{M\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{(x-t)\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M}{(x-1)}\int_{-1}^{1-(x-1)}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sqrt{1-t}}+\int_{1-(x-1)}^{1}\frac{M\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{(1-t)^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4M}{\sqrt{x-1}}.

(A.8)

Coming back to (
A.7
), these bounds prove the first part of (
A.4
).
For the second part, let
\varepsilon>0
.
Since
\theta
is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta(t)-T_{\ell}[\theta,1](t)}}\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon(1-t)^{\ell}
for any
t\in[1-\delta,1]
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta(t)-T_{\ell}[\theta,1](t)}}\right\rvert}{(x-t)(1-t)^{\ell+1}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\ell!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sup_{[-1,1]}\lvert\theta^{(\ell)}\rvert}}\right)\int_{-1}^{1-\delta}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{(x-t)(1-t)}+\varepsilon\int_{1-\delta}^{1}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)}{(x-t)(1-t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\ell!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sup_{[-1,1]}\lvert\theta^{(\ell)}\rvert}}\right)\cdot\frac{2}{\delta^{2}}+\varepsilon\cdot\frac{4M}{\sqrt{x-1}},

using (
A.8
) to bound the second term.
Bounding the second term in (
A.7
) as before, this proves the second part of (
A.4
).
∎

We also need this slight modification of Lemma
A.1
where the function
\theta
can be irregular at 0.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
[-1,1]
.
Let
\ell\geq 1
and
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
such that
\int_{-1}^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta}}\right\rvert\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty
.
Define
\Theta(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
as in (
A.2
).
Then, the following properties hold.

(i)

The function
\Theta
is in
\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
and, for any
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, (
A.3
) holds.
Moreover,
\Theta
is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
and (
A.3
) holds with
k=\ell
and
x\notin[-1,1]
.

(ii)

If
\int\frac{1}{1-t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)<\infty
, then
\Theta
is
\ell
times differentiable at 1 and (
A.3
) holds with
k=\ell
and
x=1
.

(iii)

If
\mu
has a density
\varrho
such that, for some
M>0
, for any
x\in[-1,1]
,
\varrho(x)\leq M\sqrt{1-x}
, and if, for any
x\geq 1
,
\theta(x)=T_{\ell}[\theta,1](x)
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\Theta^{(\ell)}(x)-\Theta^{(\ell)}(1)}}\right\rvert\begin{cases}\leq 8M(\ell+1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sup_{[1/2,1]}\lvert\theta^{(\ell)}\rvert}}\right)(x-1)^{1/2}&\\
\qquad{}+2^{\ell+2}(\ell+1)!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta}}\right\rvert\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\lvert\theta^{(k)}(1)\rvert}}\right)(x-1),&\text{for }x\in[1,2],\\
=o((x-1)^{1/2}),&\text{as }x\downarrow 1.\end{cases}

(A.9)

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Lemma
A.1
, only the part of the integral close to 0 has to be treated differently. We give some details below.

Part
(i)
. The part of the integral with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert t}}\right\rvert\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert/2
has to be treated differently, by bounding
1/(t-x)^{k+1}
and
T_{k}[\theta,x](t)
uniformly and using that
\int\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta}}\right\rvert\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty
.

Part
(ii)
. In the formula (
A.3
) for
\Theta^{(\ell-1)}(x)
, we cut the integral at
1/2
. The part
t\in[1/2,1]
is treated as in Lemma
A.1
and the part
t\in[-1,1/2]
is covered via bounds similar to those mentioned in Part
(i)
.

Part
(iii)
. We start from (
A.7
) and cut again the integral at
1/2
. The part
t\in[1/2,1]
is treated as in Lemma
A.1
.
The part
t\in[-1,1/2]
is at most

\displaystyle(\ell+1)!\int_{-1}^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta(t)}}\right\rvert+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}(1-t)^{k}\lvert\theta^{(k)}(1)\rvert}}\right)\frac{(x-1)}{(x-t)(1-t)^{\ell+1}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)

\displaystyle\leq(x-1)2^{\ell+2}(\ell+1)!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{-1}^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\theta(t)}}\right\rvert\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(t)+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\lvert\theta^{(k)}(1)\rvert}}\right)

and this yields the desired result.
∎

A.2
Bounds on the function
r_{\alpha}

Lemma A.3
.

Let
p>2
.
For any
\alpha\in[0,1]
, the function
r_{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
.
Moreover, there exists
C,c>0
such that for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-2}}}\right)\leq r_{\alpha}(\lambda)\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-2}}}\right),\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq\begin{cases}C\alpha&\text{if }p\leq 3,\\
C\alpha(1+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert)^{p-3}&\text{if }p>3,\end{cases}

and, if
\lambda\neq 0
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert r_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq\begin{cases}C\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-3}&\text{if }p<3,\\
C\alpha(1+(\log 1/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert)_{+})&\text{if }p=3,\\
C\alpha(1+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert)^{p-4}&\text{if }p>3.\end{cases}

Note that, when
p\leq 3
, the second derivative is exploding at 0, whereas it stays bounded in the neighbourhood of 0 if
p>3
(but possibly explodes at infinity). The function
r_{\alpha}
is actually in
\mathcal{C}^{\lceil p-2\rceil}(\mathbb{R})
but we do not try to optimize regularity statements in the case
p>3
.

Proof.

First note that
r_{\alpha}=\alpha r_{1}+(1-\alpha)r_{0}
and
r_{0}=2
, so it is enough to deal with the case
\alpha=1
. We write
r=r_{1}
for brevity.

Lower bound on
r
.

Recall from (
1.25
) that

\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\qquad r(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{V^{\prime}(\lambda)-V^{\prime}(t)}{\lambda-t}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}.

(A.10)

Note that the integrand above is positive for any
t\neq\lambda
by strict convexity of
V
, so
r(\lambda)>0
. Moreover,
V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
so, by Lemma
A.1
.
(i)
,
r
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
.
Finally, note that
r(\lambda)\sim V^{\prime}(\lambda)/(2\lambda)\sim pc_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-2}/2
as
\lambda\to\pm\infty
.
Altogether, this proves there exists
c>0
such that
r(\lambda)\geq c(1+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-2})
.

Regularity.
Since
V^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{*}
, by (
A.10
) and Lemma
A.2
.
(i)
, we get that
r
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{*}
.
On the other hand, recall (
1.26
).
Observing that the entire series has radius of convergence 1, and that the term
x\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert^{p-1}(A_{p}-B_{p}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert x}}\right\rvert)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(-1,1)
since
p>2
, we deduce that
r
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on
(-1,1)
and therefore on
\mathbb{R}
by what precedes.

Upper bounds.
From (
1.26
), noting that
B_{p}
is nonzero only if
p\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
, we see that there exists
C>0
such that, for any
\lambda\in[-1/2,1/2]
,
r(\lambda)\leq C
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert r^{\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C
and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert r^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq\begin{cases}C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-3}&\text{if }p<3,\\
C(1+(\log 1/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert)_{+})&\text{if }p=3,\\
C&\text{if }p>3.\end{cases}

Then, since
r
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{*}
, such inequalities are still true on the compact set
[-2,2]\setminus(-1/2,1/2)
(up to changing
C
).
Finally, we deal with
\lambda\notin[-2,2]
and by parity we can assume
\lambda>2
.
By Lemma
A.2
.
(i)
, we have, for any
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert r^{(k)}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{k!}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{\lvert V^{(j+1)}(\lambda)\rvert}{j!(\lambda-t)^{k+1-j}}+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert V^{\prime}(t)}}\right\rvert}{(\lambda-t)^{k+1}}}}\right)\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}\leq C(k,p)\lambda^{p-k-2},

using
\lambda-t\geq\lambda/2
and
V^{(j+1)}(\lambda)=C(j,p)\lambda^{p-j-1}
, where we write
C(j,p)
for a constant that depends on
j
and
p
only. This concludes the proof.
∎

We often use the following direct consequence of the previous lemma controlling the function
1/r_{\alpha}
and its first two derivatives.

Corollary A.4
.

Let
p>2
. There exists
C>0
such that, for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert 1/r_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq C
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert(1/r_{\alpha})^{\prime}}}\right\rvert\leq C
, and for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{r_{\alpha}}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq\begin{cases}C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-3}&\text{if }p<3,\\
C(1+(\log 1/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert)_{+})&\text{if }p=3,\\
C&\text{if }p>3.\end{cases}

A.3
Inverse of the master operator

The purpose of this subsection is to prove Lemma
4.1
concerning the existence and regularity of the preimage under the master operator
\Xi_{\alpha}
of a function.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Part
(i)
. This part of the result would follow directly from
[
9
, Lemma 3.3]
if
V
was of class
\mathcal{C}^{4}
, but here
V
is only of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
. We therefore summarize their proof below and explain where modifications are needed.
First, using singular integral equation theory
[
48
, Eq. (89.15)-(89.17)]
, they observe that
\psi_{\alpha}
as defined in (
4.1
) solves
\Xi_{\alpha}[\psi_{\alpha}]=f-a
on
(-1,1)
and therefore on
[-1,1]
by continuity.
Then, they note that the function

\varphi\colon\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{f(t)-f(\lambda)}{t-\lambda}\frac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma(t)}

is in
\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and satisfies
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\varphi}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}
by Taylor–Lagrange inequality (see Lemma
A.1
.
(i)
).
Combining this with Corollary
A.4
, we deduce that
\psi_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1])
and satisfies
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1])}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}([-1,1])}
(here they rely on
[
9
, Lemma 3.1]
which would require
V
of class
\mathcal{C}^{4}
).
Furthermore, the fact that
\Xi_{\alpha}[\psi_{\alpha}]=f-a
holds on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
follows from direct algebraic manipulations using the definition in (
4.1
).
Moreover, the function
\psi_{\alpha}
has the same regularity as
f
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
, i.e.
\mathcal{C}^{2}
, because
V_{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{*}
.

To conclude that
\psi_{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}
, it is enough to prove that
\psi_{\alpha}^{(k)}(\lambda)\to\psi_{\alpha}^{(k)}(1-)
as
\lambda\downarrow 1
for
k\in\{0,1\}
and a similar result at
-1
.
We now detail this argument
1
1

1

We follow ideas from
[
9
]
, but do not fully understand their argument, so we provide some details.
,
focusing on the behavior at
1
, the argument at
-1
being identical.
Let
\theta_{\alpha}
be equal to
\psi_{\alpha}
on
[-1,1]
and extended to
\mathbb{R}
in a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
fashion by setting
\theta_{\alpha}(\lambda)=T_{1}[\psi_{\alpha},1](\lambda)
for
\lambda>1
and
\theta_{\alpha}(\lambda)=T_{1}[\psi_{\alpha},-1](\lambda)
for
\lambda<-1
, where we recall the notation (
A.1
).
Then, for
\lambda\notin[-1,1]
, using
\theta_{\alpha}=\psi_{\alpha}
on
[-1,1]
, we have

\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)=\frac{\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\theta_{\alpha}(t)}{\lambda-t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(t)+f(\lambda)-a}{\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{1}{\lambda-t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(t)-\frac{1}{2}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda)}=\theta_{\alpha}(\lambda)+\frac{f(\lambda)-a-\Xi_{\alpha}[\theta_{\alpha}](\lambda)}{\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{1}{\lambda-t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(t)-\frac{1}{2}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda)}\eqqcolon\theta_{\alpha}(\lambda)+\frac{F(\lambda)}{G(\lambda)},

(A.11)

where we introduced a shorthand for the numerator and the denominator.
It is now enough to prove that
(F/G)^{(k)}(\lambda)\to 0
as
\lambda\downarrow 1
for
k\in\{0,1\}
.
Recalling the definition of
\Xi_{\alpha}
in (
1.27
) and applying Lemma
A.1
with
\mu=\mu_{V_{\alpha}}
,
\theta=\theta_{\alpha}
and
\ell=1
, we deduce that the function
F
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on
(1,\infty)
, differentiable at 1 and
F^{\prime}(\lambda)=F^{\prime}(1)+o((\lambda-1)^{1/2})
as
\lambda\downarrow 1
(for the term
\frac{1}{2}\theta_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}
, note that
\theta_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}=0
on
(1,\infty)
and that
V_{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{*}
).
But the function
F
is zero on
[-1,1]
, because
\Xi_{\alpha}[\theta_{\alpha}]=\Xi_{\alpha}[\psi_{\alpha}]=f+a
on
[-1,1]
, so in particular
F(1)=F^{\prime}(1)=0
.
Combining these facts, we get

F(\lambda)=o((\lambda-1)^{3/2})\qquad\text{and}\qquad F^{\prime}(\lambda)=o((\lambda-1)^{1/2}),\qquad\text{as }\lambda\downarrow 1.

(A.12)

On the other hand, for
\lambda>1
,
G(\lambda)
can be rewritten in the following way, by definitions of
s_{V_{\alpha}}
and
g_{\alpha}
and then using (
2.15
),

G(\lambda)=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{1}{\lambda-t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}(t)-\frac{1}{2}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\lambda)=-s_{V_{\alpha}}(\lambda)-\frac{g_{\alpha}(\lambda)}{2\lambda}=-r_{\alpha}(\lambda)\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-1}.

(A.13)

But, by Corollary
A.4
,
1/r_{\alpha}
and its derivative are bounded on
\mathbb{R}
. So, combining this with (
A.12
), we deduce that
(F/G)^{(k)}(\lambda)\to 0
as
\lambda\downarrow 1
for
k\in\{0,1\}
and this concludes the proof that
\psi_{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}
.

It remains to show
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}
. We have already seen that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1])}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}([-1,1])}
.
On
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-2,2]
, we use the expression of
\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)
in
(
4.1
), where the denominator can be rewritten as in (
A.13
). Then, using Corollary
A.4
again, we get

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-2,2])}\leq C(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}([-1,1])}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a}}\right\rvert)\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}},

noting that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert a}}\right\rvert\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\infty}
.
Now we consider
\lambda\in[1,2]
, the case
\lambda\in[-2,-1]
being identical.
Here we use the expression of
\psi_{\alpha}(\lambda)
given in (
A.11
).
First, by definition of
\theta_{\alpha}
, we get
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\theta_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}([1,2])}\leq C(\psi_{\alpha}(1)+\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(1))\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}
.
Then, using Lemma
A.1
again, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert F^{\prime}(\lambda)-F^{\prime}(1)}}\right\rvert\leq(\lambda-1)\cdot\sup_{(1,2]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f+\frac{1}{2}\theta_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rvert+C(\lambda-1)^{1/2}\cdot\sup_{[-1,1]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}(\lambda-1)^{1/2},

and, together with
F(1)=F^{\prime}(1)=0
, this yields

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert F(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}(\lambda-1)^{3/2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert F^{\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}(\lambda-1)^{1/2},\qquad\text{for any }\lambda\in[1,2].

(A.14)

Combining this with the expression of
G(\lambda)
in (
A.13
) and Corollary
A.4
, we get
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert F/G}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}([1,2])}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}
, which concludes the proof of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}
.

Part
(ii)
. The argument is similar to Part
(i)
.
We now have
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\varphi}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}}
, which, together with Corollary
A.4
, shows that
\psi_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}([-1,1]\setminus\{0\})
and

\forall\lambda\in(-1,1)\setminus\{0\},\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}}\begin{cases}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert^{p-3}&\text{if }p<3,\\
(1+(\log 1/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert)_{+})&\text{if }p=3,\\
1&\text{if }p>3.\end{cases}

(A.15)

On
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
, the function
\psi_{\alpha}
still has the same regularity as
f
, that is
\mathcal{C}^{3}
. The regularity of
\psi_{\alpha}
at
\pm 1
follows from the same lines, but we apply Lemma
A.2
instead of Lemma
A.1
, because
\psi_{\alpha}
, and hence
\theta_{\alpha}
, is not
\mathcal{C}^{2}
at 0.
This shows that
F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)=F^{\prime\prime}(1)+o((\lambda-1)^{1/2})
as
\lambda\downarrow 1
and it follows similarly that
(F/G)^{(k)}(\lambda)\to 0
as
\lambda\downarrow 1
for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
, which proves
\psi_{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
at
1
.
Moreover, Lemma
A.2
also yields, for
\lambda\in[1,2]
,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)-F^{\prime\prime}(1)}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq(\lambda-1)\cdot\sup_{(1,2]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f+\frac{1}{2}\theta_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}^{\prime}}}\right)^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\right\rvert+C(\lambda-1)^{1/2}\cdot\sup_{[1/2,1]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad{}+C(\lambda-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{\alpha}}+\sum_{k=0}^{2}\lvert\psi_{\alpha}^{(k)}(1)\rvert}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}}(\lambda-1)^{1/2},

using (
A.15
) and that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\psi_{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1])}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}([-1,1])}
by Part
(i)
.
Together with Corollary
A.4
, it follows that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert f}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}}
for
\lambda\in[1,2]
, and so for
\lambda\in[-2,-1]
as well.
The same bound but for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[-2,2]
is done as in Part
(i)
.
∎

A.4
Bounds on
f_{\alpha,z}
and its preimage via the master operator

This section contains the proofs of Lemmas
4.2
and
4.3
.
Recall that
g(t)=tV^{\prime}(t)=pc_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert t}}\right\rvert^{p}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
We first establish a preliminary result containing bounds on the function
g
, which are improved versions of the Taylor–Lagrange inequality (note that
g^{\prime\prime\prime}
is unbounded when
p<3
).

Lemma A.5
.

Let
p>2
. There exists
C>0
such that, for any
t,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert g(t)-g(\lambda)-(t-\lambda)g^{\prime}(\lambda)-\frac{(t-\lambda)^{2}}{2}g^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert t-\lambda}}\right\rvert^{3}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda}}\right\rvert\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert t}}\right\rvert)^{p-3},

(A.16)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert g^{\prime}(t)-g^{\prime}(\lambda)-(t-\lambda)g^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)}}\right\rvert
